Step in Tine: Forking Processes in Functional Choreographies
Ashley Samuelson, Andrew K. Hirsch, Ethan Cecchetti

Abstract
. Traditional concurrent-programming techniques require programmers to painstakingly write programs for each participant in a concurrent system. Choreographic programming, in contrast, allows a programmer to write one centralized program and compile it to individual programs. This approach simplifies critical properties like deadlock freedom, but it complicates forking new processes , a core primitive in concurrent programming. This work addresses that gap with the choreographic fork calculus λ \lambda ⋔ \pitchfork , the first functional choreographic language with process forking. λ \lambda ⋔ \pitchfork provides a deadlock-freedom guarantee while allowing programs to dynamically determine when to spawn new processes, what they will do, and who will communicate with them. In doing so, it supports practical applications like load balancers and parallel divide-and-conquer.

1.
Introduction

As nearly every computer system has come to rely on parallelism for efficiency,
the difficulty of writing correct concurrent code has become an increasing concern.
Complex interactions between processes can lead to subtle bugs such as deadlocks,
where two or more processes are waiting on each other, preventing the system from progressing.

Choreographic programming

(
Montesi 2023
)
has recently emerged as a promising tool to address this challenge.
Instead of writing separate programs for each process,
the choreographic paradigm allows programmers to specify the behavior and interactions of all processes in a single, top-level program called a choreography.
A compiler then produces code for each process using a procedure called
endpoint projection
(EPP).
This global specification allows programmers to reason about the system as a whole and leads to
deadlock freedom by design
,
eliminating a common source of bugs in concurrent programs.

In the beginning, choreographic programming languages formed a promising foundation, but lacked language features necessary to use the paradigm in modern software engineering
(
Montesi 2013
;
Carbone and Montesi 2013
)
.
A flurry of recent work has added capabilities including higher-order programming
(
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2022
;
Hirsch and Garg 2022
;
Giallorenzo et al. 2023
)
,
process polymorphism
(
Graversen et al. 2024
;
Samuelson et al. 2025
)
, and multiply-located values
(
Bates et al. 2025
;
Samuelson et al. 2025
)
.
While these features have made choreographic programming more expressive, they still lack critical capabilities, including the ability to dynamically
fork processes
.

Early choreographic work enabled process spawning
(
Carbone and Montesi 2013
;
Cruz-Filipe and Montesi 2017b
)
,
but relied on a feature-poor calculus, lacking data structures, closures, and even simple variable binding.
These omissions do not merely simplify presentation;
they are critical to ensuring deadlock freedom without a type system or other powerful static analysis.
This work develops a type system to manage spawned processes,
allowing us to build an expressive language with many modern functional-programming features, while still providing a deadlock-freedom guarantee.
Moreover, first-class process names
(
Samuelson et al. 2025
)
facilitate writing concurrent programs in the intuitive style provided by choreographies,
even when those programs rely on dynamic process spawning.

Enabling dynamic process forking raises three important safety and implementation concerns.

(1)

Existing processes must be made aware of a new process so they can interact with it.

(2)

Executing processes must not attempt to communicate with a terminated process.

(3)

New processes must know what code to execute, and the code must match the expectations of existing processes to avoid deadlock.

Problem 1 can be resolved utilizing first-class process names
(
Samuelson et al. 2025
)
,
a form of higher-order communication allowing a parent process to transmit the name of a newly-spawned child across the network.
Problem 2 is fundamentally a resource-management problem.
Spawned processes are resources.
They should be released when they are no longer needed, and any attempt to use them after they are released will cause critical failures—in this context, deadlock.
We solve this problem by lifting the principles of type-based resource management to process polymorphism,
similar to the invention of
located types

t@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
to represent data of type
t
at the location
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
in functional choreographies
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
;
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2022
)
.

Problem 3 requires more careful analysis.
New processes must know what code to execute, so their parent must have access to that code to initialize them.
To properly project the parent’s code from a choreography, endpoint projection (EPP) must be able to statically determine what code the child will need.
If the child has an unbounded dynamic lifetime, identifying this code statically is an undecidable problem.
One might give the child the entire choreography,
but avoiding deadlock from mismatched expectations with existing processes would then pose a serious challenge.

A key insight of this work is that we can resolve Problem 3 using a simple and well understood resource-management technique:

syntactically scoping
process lifetimes.
With lexical lifetimes,
the code for a spawned thread is just the projection of the choreographic code where the thread’s name is in-scope—a straightforward compile-time determination.
However, care must be taken to prevent
spawned process names—references to scoped resources—from escaping their scope and causing deadlock.
Closures, in particular, complicate this task.
Consider the following choreographies returning closures that outsource a computation provided by a client
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
to a worker.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{runThread}=\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\lambda}~\mathsf{task}\ldotp\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{~}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()\\
\alpha.\mathsf{task}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}\mathsf{task}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\alpha\end{array}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}&\alpha.(\mathsf{task}~())\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\end{array}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{runThreadBad}=\\
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\quad\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{~}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}let}}&{\alpha}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}C}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}fork}}()}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}in}}&{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\lambda}~\mathsf{task}\ldotp\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{~}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}let}}&{\alpha.\mathsf{task}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{task}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rightsquigarrow}}\alpha}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}in}}&{\alpha.(\mathsf{task}~())\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}C}}\end{array}}\end{array}\end{array}

Both take a
\mathsf{task}
owned by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
and send it to a worker
\alpha
spawned by the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()
operation,
which executes the task and sends the results back to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
.
However, they differ critically in the scope of the spawned worker.
The first implementation,
\mathsf{runThread}
, is a function that, on application, spawns
\alpha
, executes the task, and then terminates
\alpha
as it falls out of scope.
The second implementation, however, spawns the worker
first
and returns a function that simply closes over its name.
Since
\alpha
has a lexical lifetime, it terminates before the closure is ever called, never performing any action.
If
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
later invokes the closure (e.g.,
\mathsf{runThreadBad}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lambda}\_\ldotp 1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}1)
),
it will deadlock due to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
attempting to send a message to a worker process that is no longer running.

This work develops
\lambda

\pitchfork

(pronounced “lambda-fork”), the first functional choreographic language to allow dynamic process spawning.
To avoid deadlocks from process names escaping their scope,

\lambda

\pitchfork

uses a type system to disallow code like
\mathsf{runThreadBad}
while allowing code like
\mathsf{runThread}
.
The type system tracks which processes might participate in each subexpression
and tags function types with a set of
latent participants
to describe which existing locations—those not spawned in the body of the function—might be required to fully execute the function.
For instance, the latent participants in the closure returned by
\mathsf{runThread}
are just
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
,
while those for the closure in
\mathsf{runThreadBad}
are
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
and
\alpha
.
The type system then prohibits spawned process names from escaping their scope
even as latent participants
.
This final restriction correctly deems
\mathsf{runThreadBad}
ill-typed.

Spawning processes also complicates proving deadlock freedom.
The only prior deadlock-freedom proof supporting multiply-located values relied on
a bisimulation between the choreography and its projection that, in turn, relied on extensive implicit synchronization
(
Samuelson et al. 2025
)
.
In
\lambda

\pitchfork

, such synchronization would not only negate many benefits of parallelism,
it would require locations who do not even know each other exist to synchronize.
To avoid this conundrum, we define a weaker correspondence that allows us to give
the first proof of deadlock freedom for a choreographic language with multiply-located values and no implicit synchronization.

The rest of the paper is structured as follows.
Section
2
reviews background, and Section
3
defines the underlying system model.
The subsequent Sections present the following main contributions.

•

Section
4
introduces
\lambda

\pitchfork

,
a functional choreographic language supporting process spawning.

•

Section
4.3
formulates a type system for
\lambda

\pitchfork

that tracks which processes might participate in a choreography
to ensure that no process needs to perform computation after it dies.

•

Section
6
defines endpoint projection (EPP),
a procedure to compile a choreography into a target-language program (Section
5
) for each process,
and characterizes EPP’s correctness with respect to a top-level operational semantics.
This result combines with the soundness of the type system to prove that executing a projected system will never cause a deadlock—the first such proof for a choreography with multiply-located values and no implicit synchronization.

Finally, Section
7
reviews related work, and Section
8
concludes.

2.
Background

To better situate the contributions of our work, we first review the design, features, and limitations of prior choreographic programming languages.

2.1.
Functional Choreographies

Our language
\lambda

\pitchfork

primarily extends
\lambda
qc

(
Samuelson et al. 2025
)
, which in turn extends Pirouette
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
)
, the first functional choreographic programming language.
Like most choreographic languages,
\lambda

\pitchfork

prefixes each local operation with the process that performs it.
For example, to specify that location
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
should compute
1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}3
and send the result to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
, one would write

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
.
To differentiate operations, we write source programs using a
sans-serif
font,
with location constants in
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}red}
,
local operations in
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}green}
,
and choreographic operations in
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}blue}}
.
Local programs such as “
1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}3
” can be specified by a user-chosen language, so long as it is equipped with
a substitution-based operational semantics, a sound type system, and its values can be shared via message-passing.
Choreographic constructs such as send (
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}
), on the other hand, are fixed.

While the choreographic and local operations are separate, the choreography can
sequence local computations using features such as
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}
-expressions.
For example, the output of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
is an integer located at
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
,
which can then be used in a subsequent local computation at
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
by binding the result to a (local) variable
x
as follows:

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(x\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}-}}2)}
.

Choreography-level control flow is supported by the expression
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}if}}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}then}}~{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}else}}~{C_{2}}
,
where
C
evaluates to a boolean value known to some process
\ell
.
As others outside of
\ell
cannot see the output of
C
, they cannot determine which branch to execute.
To allow other locations to participate in this branch,
\lambda

\pitchfork

includes a
selection statement

{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}C
in which
\ell
communicates the chosen branching
direction
d\in\{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}},\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\}
of
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}
eft or
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}
ight to all locations in the set
\rho
.

Like
\lambda
qc
,
\lambda

\pitchfork

supports
multiply-located values
(MLVs)
(
Bates et al. 2025
;
Sweet et al. 2023
)
,
which are local values known to multiple locations and ensure all parties agree, in addition to selection statements.
For instance,
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.(3\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}>}}1)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}

first instructs
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
to compute the local operation
3\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}>}}1
,
and then instructs
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
to send the result to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
.
The result is the multiply-located value
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}
.
MLVs can be used as an alternative to synchronization messages for branching.
For instance, the following choreography ensures all three locations branch in the same direction:

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}if}}_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}}~{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.(3\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}>}}1)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}then}}~{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}else}}~{C_{2}}
.
Note that when using MLVs, it often becomes necessary to annotate e.g., who is sending a value or participating in an
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}if}}
expression.

Endpoint Projection

Like most choreographic languages,
\lambda

\pitchfork

defines a compilation procedure called
endpoint projection
(EPP) that translates a choreography into a separate program for each participant.
EPP is a syntax-guided translation that extracts the actions that a single location needs to perform from the choreography.
The location being projected to is denoted by a subscript to the compilation operator, as in
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}
and
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
.
For instance, consider the choreography

C=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}*}}4)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(3\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}

in which
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
each compute a value and then send it to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
.
The only actions that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
need to perform are computing the value and sending it, while
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
needs to receive both values:

\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({2\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}*}}4})}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}}\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({3\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2})}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}}\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}}}=\mkern 2.0mu\begin{array}[m]{@{}l@{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}\\
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}\end{array}

The target (network) language is written using an
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}orange~teletype}}
font.

\lambda

\pitchfork

also defines a top-level operational semantics directly on choreographies, allowing developers to reason about the behavior of the system as a whole.
This choreographic semantics allows for out-of-order execution,
so long as the order of operations for each individual location is respected.
For instance, note that if we execute the projected programs shown above concurrently,
either
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
or
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
could perform their local computation first,
but
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
must receive the values in the specified order.
This means that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
can compute 8 and 5, respectively, in any order in choreography
C
, even though
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
’s computation is after the semicolon.

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
, conversely, must receive 8 before 5.
The top-level choreographic semantics allows any of these orderings.

As this top-level semantics provides a separate interpretation from the EPP-based one,
it is important that their results are equivalent.

Samuelson et al. 2025
show that these two semantics are bisimilar for
\lambda
qc
, and so will always produce the same value.
Besides allowing developers to soundly reason about the execution of a system using the top-level semantics,
this property also guarantees that any concurrent execution of a projected choreography is deadlock-free,
meaning that no process will wait indefinitely for a message that will never arrive
due to a mismatch between the expected send and receive operations.

2.2.
Process Polymorphism

Process polymorphism
(
Graversen et al. 2024
)
allows choreographies to abstract over their participants.
Analogously to type polymorphism, process polymorphism in both
\lambda
qc
and
\lambda

\pitchfork

is implemented with a process abstraction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\Lambda}{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\ldotp{}}{C}
,
where variable
\ell
represents a generic process name bound in the scope of
C
,
allowing it to be instantiated with different participants.
For example, a programmer could write a process function in which
\ell
computes the sum of two numbers and sends the result to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
as
F={\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\Lambda}{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\ldotp{}}{\ell.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}
, and later instantiate
\ell
to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
with the syntax
F~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
.

While process polymorphism allows for choreographies to be instantiated with different participants,
it does not—on its own—allow for the names of processes to be treated in a first-class manner.
First-class process names
(
Sweet et al. 2023
;
Samuelson et al. 2025
)
solve this problem
by allowing local computations to generate, examine, and send process names as values.
For example, the expression
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}if}~{e}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}then}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}else}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}})
selects between
the process names
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
based on the value of the boolean
e
known to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
.
The output of this computation can then be shared with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
so they are aware of
who should perform the subsequent computation, and bound to a variable
\ell
using
\lambda

\pitchfork

’s
type-let expression
(inherited from
\lambda
qc
) as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{~}l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}&{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}.\ell}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}if}~{e}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}then}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}else}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}&{\ell.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}3)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}\end{array}

3.
System Model

\lambda

\pitchfork

assumes the underlying system contains a set of potential computational units (threads, processes, etc.),
which we refer to interchangeably as
locations
or
processes
,
and each has a unique name from a space
\mathcal{L}
.
We use the term
thread
to refer to a dynamically spawned location that will die within the run of the program.
The number of locations executing at any given time is finite,
but programs may spawn an unbounded number of new locations and each name must be unique, so
\mathcal{L}
must be infinite.

As noted in Section
2.1
,
\lambda

\pitchfork

follows Pirouette
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
)
and
\lambda
qc

(
Samuelson et al. 2025
)

in allowing local programs to be specified in nearly any language.
This
local language
must only satisfy a set of rules common to most expression-based languages.
Our assumptions on the local language are nearly identical to those in
\lambda
qc
and Pirouette, although we make some generalizations.

3.1.
Local Language

Operational Semantics

We require that the local language be presented as a set of expressions coupled with a small-step operational semantics, a distinguished set of values, and a type system.
We write
e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e_{2}
to denote that the expression
e_{1}
steps to
e_{2}
in the local language’s operational semantics.
The semantics must satisfy the following two properties.

(1)

Values cannot step: if
\operatorname{Val}({v})
, then there is no
e
such that
v\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e
.

(2)

The semantics is confluent:
if
e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}e_{2}
and
e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}e_{3}
,
then there is some
e_{4}
such that
e_{2}\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}e_{4}
and
e_{3}\mathrel{\longrightarrow^{*}}e_{4}
.

Property (
1
) is a standard assumption about the set of values in the language,
and property (
2
) ensures multiply-located computations produce the same result
at each location they execute at.
Property (
2
) loosens the requirement in
\lambda
qc
that the local semantics must satisfy the diamond property,
allowing a wider variety of languages and evaluation strategies (e.g., full
\beta
-reduction) to be used for local computations.

Local Type System

Just as the syntax of a choreography depends on the syntax of the local language,
the choreographic type system depends on the local language specifying a type system.
The local type system must include both a kinding judgment and a typing judgement.
This allows, but does not require, the local type system to be polymorphic.
The type system must also be sound with respect to the operational semantics.
Specifically, it should satisfy the standard progress and preservation properties,
and additionally respect well-formed (type) substitutions.

We denote a local kinding judgment
\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\Vdash}t\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}::}*_{e}
.
We assume for simplicity there is a single local kind
*_{e}
, but our results generalize to languages with multiple kinds.
We denote local typing judgements
\Gamma;\Delta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\Vdash}e\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}:}t

where
\Gamma
is again a kinding context and
\Delta
is a typing context.
To distinguish these judgments from the choreographic type system, we use a
green
double-vertical turnstile
\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\Vdash}
.

To support choreographic control-flow branching, we generalize Pirouette and
\lambda
qc
.
Instead of requiring a local boolean type, we allow an arbitrary (user-defined) predicate
\text{isSum}({s},{t_{1}},{t_{2}})

indicating that every value of type
s
can be interpreted as either a
t_{1}
or a
t_{2}
.
For instance, the local language can specify
\text{isSum}({\mathsf{bool}},{\mathsf{unit}},{\mathsf{unit}})
and interpret
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}
as
\operatorname{inl}{()}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}false}
as
\operatorname{inr}{()}
.
The only requirement is that there is a deterministic partial function
\mathrm{getCase}
called the
extraction function
.
We require that, if
\text{isSum}({s},{t_{1}},{t_{2}})
and
\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\Vdash}v\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}:}s
for a value
v
,
then either
\mathrm{getCase}(v)=\operatorname{inl}(v_{1})
with
\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\Vdash}v_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}:}t_{1}

or
\mathrm{getCase}(v)=\operatorname{inr}(v_{2})
with
\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\Vdash}v_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}:}t_{2}
.
On other expressions, it may be undefined.

We support first-class process names identically to
\lambda
qc
.
Specifically, the local language has two types
\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}
and
\mathsf{locset}_{\rho}

defining first-class
representations
of location names and sets of locations, respectively.
We write representations using the syntax
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}
—denoting local values—to distinguish them from the actual location
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\in\mathcal{L}
or location set
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}\subseteq\mathcal{L}
—which are type-expressions.
While the local language may use any underlying data type (e.g., (sets of) strings) for these values,
as with the sum types above, the local language must be able to uniquely reify any well-typed representation into a corresponding kind.

There is also an additional soundness requirement—inherited from
\lambda
qc
—that the sets
\rho
in the types
\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}
and
\mathsf{locset}_{\rho}
provide an upper-bound on the set of locations to which an expression of that type might resolve to.
As an example, we could assign both
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}if}~{e}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}then}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}else}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}}
to the type
\mathsf{loc}_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}
, but
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}
cannot be assigned this type.
Since a spawned thread could potentially take any name, we also require each location
L\in\mathcal{L}
to have at least one representation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}

to ensure choreographies do not become stuck when spawning a thread.
We do not require there to be any representation for a given set of locations, however, as this is not a safety concern.

Many
\lambda
-calculi already satisfy our requirements.
As our requirements strictly generalize those of
\lambda
qc
, all local languages from that work are applicable.
Here we present an examplar local langauge extending System F that we use in examples throughout this work.

Example 1 (System F).

\begin{array}[]{@{} >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}c >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l @{}}\textrm{Types}&t&\Coloneqq&\alpha\,\mid\,\mathsf{unit}\,\mid\,\mathsf{int}\,\mid\,\mathsf{string}\,\mid\,\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}\,\mid\,\mathsf{locset}_{\rho}\\
&&\,\mid&t_{1}\to t_{2}\,\mid\,t_{1}+t_{2}\,\mid\,t_{1}\times t_{2}\,\mid\,\forall\alpha\ldotp t\,\mid\,\mu\alpha\ldotp t\\
\textrm{Expressions}&e&\Coloneqq&x\,\mid\,()\,\mid\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\mid\,s\in\mathit{str}\,\mid\,\operatorname{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}fun}}{f}({x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\coloneqq}}{e}\,\mid\,e_{1}~e_{2}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\Lambda}\alpha\ldotp e\,\mid\,e~t\\
&&\,\mid&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{e}\,\mid\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{e}\,\mid\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}case}~{e}~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}of}~({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}}\Rightarrow{e_{1}})~({\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}}\Rightarrow{e_{2}})\\
&&\,\mid&(e_{1},e_{2})\,\mid\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}fst}~{e}\,\mid\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}snd}~{e}\,\mid\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}fold}~{e}\,\mid\,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}unfold}~{e}\\
&&\,\mid&e_{1}\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}e_{2}\,\mid\,e_{1}\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}*}}e_{2}\,\mid\,e_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}=}}e_{2}\,\mid\,e_{1}\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}<}}e_{2}\end{array}

Figure 1.
Example Local Language Syntax.

System F extended with algebraic and recursive data types, primitive integers, and primitive strings representing locations,
satisfies the requirements for local languages.
To implement sum types, we define
\text{isSum}({s},{t_{1}},{t_{2}})
to hold precisely when
s=t_{1}+t_{2}
(syntactically),
and define the extraction function as

\mathrm{getCase}(e)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\operatorname{inl}(v)&e=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{v}\\
\operatorname{inr}(v)&e=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{v}\\
\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Since we do not require that all local expressions terminate, there is no issue
with including named recursive functions and unrestricted recursive types.
We use lists of strings (defined using recursive data types) to represent location sets.
Having multiple list permutations represent the same set of locations is also not a concern,
since we do not require representations to be unique.
The syntax of this potential local language is shown in Figure
1
.

4.
The
\lambda

\pitchfork

Language

We now present
\lambda

\pitchfork

, the first functional choreographic programming language that can dynamically spawn threads.
As previously mentioned, we inherit the core of our language from
\lambda
qc

(
Samuelson et al. 2025
)
—including
features such as algebraic and recursive data types, multiply-located local computations, process polymorphism, and first-class process names—and
retain the traditional deadlock-freedom guarantee of choreographic languages.

4.1.
\lambda

\pitchfork

Syntax

\begin{array}[]{@{} >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}c >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l @{}}\textrm{Selection Labels}&d&\Coloneqq&\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}\,\mid\,\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\\
\textrm{Choreographies}&C&\Coloneqq&X\,\mid\,\rho.e\\
&&\,\mid&\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}}\,\mid\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}}\\
&&\,\mid&C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho\,\mid\,{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}C\\
&&\,\mid&\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fun}}}_{\rho}{F}({X\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mu\tau})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\,\mid\,C_{1}\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}C_{2}\,\mid\,\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}tfun}}}_{\rho}{F}({\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\,\mid\,C\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}t\\
&&\,\mid&\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{C_{1}})~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}})\\
&&\,\mid&\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inl}}_{\rho}~{C}\,\mid\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inr}}_{\rho}~{C}\,\mid\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}case}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}~(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inl}}~{X}\Rightarrow{C_{1}})~(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inr}}~{Y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}})\\
&&\,\mid&\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fold}}_{\rho}~{C}\,\mid\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}unfold}}_{\rho}~{C}\,\mid\,(C_{1},C_{2})_{\rho}\,\mid\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fst}}_{\rho}~{C}\,\mid\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}snd}}_{\rho}~{C}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\ell}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}\,\mid\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C}\end{array}

Figure 2.
Syntax of Choreographies in
\lambda

\pitchfork

.

Figure
2
presents the full syntax of
\lambda

\pitchfork

.
To cleanly separate choreographic data, local data, and types,
we write choreographic program variables in uppercase Roman characters (
X,Y,F,\dotsc
),
local program variables in lowercase Roman characters (
x,y,f,\dotsc
),
and type variables in lowercase Greek characters (
\alpha,\beta,\dotsc
).
The metavariable
\ell
denotes a location,
\rho
a set of locations,
\tau
a choreographic type,
t_{e}
a local type,
\kappa
a kind, and
t
a type of any kind.
To support polymorphism, these metavariables all represent a type- or kind-expression (e.g., the set
\rho=\alpha\cup\{\beta,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\}
is valid),
the syntax of which we define in Section
4.3
.

Most constructs in
\lambda

\pitchfork

are standard for a functional language, consisting of operations on data types
appropriately generalized to choreographies, but there are some key differences.
The expression
\rho.e
denotes a local program
e
that is executed by all locations in the (non-empty) set
\rho
.
In cases where
\rho=\{\ell\}
is a singleton, we use the shorthand
\ell.e
.
Local programs like
e
can use variables bound in the scope of the choreography, which are prepended with the location(s) that bind(s) them.
For instance,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x
denotes variable
x
in the namespace of location
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
,
which is distinct from a variable
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.x
in the namespace of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
, and this is reflected in our substitution semantics.
If a local variable is bound in the scope of multiple locations, we write
\rho.x
to mean that
x
is in the namespace of all locations in
\rho
.
Local variables (resp. type variables) can be bound to the result of a choreography using a let expression
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}}
(resp.
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}}
).
In both of these expressions,
\rho
may be a subset of the locations who know the output of
C_{1}
.

Data can be shared between locations using the operation
C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho
,
in which the output of choreography
C
is sent by
\ell
to all locations in the set
\rho
via message passing.
The output of
C
must be a local value known to
\ell
, although it may also be known to others.
For notational simplicity, we elide the
\ell
and write
C\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\rho
when
C
is known only to
\ell
.
Since the sender, all recipients, and anyone else who knew the value of a message
will all agree on it afterward, the semantics of sends are
collecting
—the output is a value located at all relevant locations.
For instance, the send
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.(4\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}-}}2)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}D}\}
results in the multiply-located value
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}D}\}.2
.
A separate use of message passing is
selection
statements
{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}C
, which can be used to synchronize on the branch
d\in\{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}},\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\}
taken in a case-expression—described below—with the locations in
\rho^{\prime}
.

Named recursive functions are written as
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fun}}}_{\rho}{F}({X\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mu\tau})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}
, where
F
is the name
of the function,
X
is its argument, and
C
is the body of the function.
The annotation
\rho
is the set of locations who are required to know the function definition;
other locations do not know its definition.
This requirement is dually reflected in the syntax for function application, written
C_{1}\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}C_{2}
,
which contains the infix application notation
\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}
to convey that only those locations in
\rho
need to perform the application.
If the function
F
is not recursively-defined (i.e.,
F
is not free in
C
), we use the notation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\lambda}_{\rho}X\ldotp C
.
As with message sending syntax, we elide the
\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}
when
\rho
is clear from context.

Polymorphism is implemented in
\lambda

\pitchfork

using type functions and type applications.
Type functions, written
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}tfun}}}_{\rho}{F}({\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}
, are similar to the type abstractions
\Lambda\alpha\ldotp C

found in System F and previous chreographies
(
Samuelson et al. 2025
;
Graversen et al. 2024
)
, but allow the function
F
to be recursively defined,
abstracting over any kind
\kappa
of the language.
Similarly to standard functions, the set
\rho
tracks which locations know the definition of the type function.
Type applications, written
C\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}t
, mirror function applications explained above.
As with standard functions, when
F
is not free in
C
we use the notation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\Lambda}_{\rho}\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa\ldotp C
.

When a type function abstracts over a location (set),
it may be applied to any location.
For instance,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\Lambda}\ell\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}}\ldotp\ell.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}1)
can be applied to both
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
or
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
,
producing an integer at the respective locations.
As we cannot a-priori know who the function will be applied to,
we therefore require that
all locations know the definition
of every process-polymorphic type function
(abstracting over a kind
\kappa\in\{*_{\mathsf{loc}},*_{\mathsf{locset}},*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}\}
)
declared in the scope of their lifetime.
In this case, we elide the
\rho
and write
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\Lambda}\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa\ldotp C
.
Since who must perform the application depends on who the function is applied to,
we still utilize the syntax
C\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}t
for applications in this case.

\lambda

\pitchfork

includes two separate forms of branching: local case-expressions and choreographic case-expressions.
Local case-expressions, written
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{C_{1}})~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}})
, generalize the if-expressions
found in prior work
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
;
Samuelson et al. 2025
;
Graversen et al. 2024
;
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2022
, e.g.,)
, and branch the choreography on the result of a local computation.
Here
C
must produce a value of local type
t
known to
\rho
where
\text{isSum}({t},{t_{1}},{t_{2}})
holds.
The local variables
x
and
y
are bound for all locations in
\rho
with types
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, respectively.
If the scrutinee
C
is a boolean and
x
and
y
are not free in the branches, we use the syntax
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}if}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}then}}~{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}else}}~{C_{2}}
.
To inform additional locations
\rho^{\prime}
which branch is taken, a programmer has two options.
First, they can share the value of
C
using the send operation
C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho^{\prime}
and branch on the resulting collected value.
Alternatively, they can include selection statements
{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}C
in the branches
to inform locations in
\rho^{\prime}
which branch was taken.
In the second case, the value of
C
is not available to the additional locations,
which may be desirable for security or performance reasons.

Choreographic case-expressions, written
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}case}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}~(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inl}}~{X}\Rightarrow{C_{1}})~(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inr}}~{Y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}})
, are conceptually similar to local case-expressions,
but instead branch the choreography on a choreographic sum—either of the form
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inl}}_{\rho}~{V_{1}}
or
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inr}}_{\rho}~{V_{2}}
.
This means that
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
could be data of a more complex type, such as a choreographic pair or list containing multiple local values.
Importantly, unlike in local case-expressions, the
location of the data may differ
between the two cases,
so long as all locations that the data may appear at are contained in
\rho
, and thus know whether the value is
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inl}}
or
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inr}}
.
Selection statements can also be used in the branches of choreographic
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}case}}
-expressions to allow locations outside of
\rho
to know which branch to take.

Similar to
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}case}}
-expressions, choreographic pairs and recursive data types act like their usual functional-programming counterparts,
but with an annotation
\rho
describing who knows about the data.
As with other such annotations, we elide them when they are clear from context.

Fork and Kill Expressions

The key addition of
\lambda

\pitchfork

is the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expression, which allows dynamic spawning of new locations.
Specifically, the expression
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\ell}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}
instructs location
\ell
to spawn a child process
and binds its name to the type variable
\alpha
, allowing the new location to perform computations in the body
C
.
The variable
x
is bound at locations
\alpha
and
\ell
to a first-class local representation of the name
\alpha
,
allowing
\ell
to notify other locations of the new child and facilitating direct communication between
\alpha
and any other location.

We include two notational shortcuts for
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
.
First, if
x
is not free in
C
, we simplify the binding and write
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\alpha}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\ell}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}
.
Second, the notation
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\alpha}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\ell}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}
is sugar for

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\_,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\ell.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\alpha}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C})}

which shares the name
\alpha
of the newly spawned process with everyone in
\rho
, as well as
\ell
and
\alpha
itself.

The construct
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C}
serves as a dual to the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expression,
and is used to track which threads are currently spawned and differentiate them from other non-ephemeral processes.
Informally, this is an administrative term used by the operational semantics to track thread lifetimes;
it is not intended to be in the surface language used by programmers.
It serves to make the lexical scope of the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
construct explicit during reduction,
providing the syntactic handle necessary for the type system and EPP to track the precise lifetime of the thread.
Specifically, when a new thread
L
is spawned by a
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expression with body
C
, the body will simply be placed within
a
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
expression while executing to denote the fact that
L
will die once
C
finishes execution.

Example
2
, shown below, demonstrates how the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expression
can be used in tandem with other language features such as case-expressions, process polymorphism, and the type-let expression.

Example 2 (Load Balancer).

Consider a cloud computing application where a client
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
wishes to outsource an expensive computation
F
with input
X
.
The below function
\mathsf{runWithWorker}
shows how
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
can run
F
on a generic worker
W
using process polymorphism.
Once the worker has computed
F~X
, they will inform a manager process
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}
, who will execute a callback function
\mathsf{onFinish}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{runWithWorker}~W~F~X~\mathsf{onFinish}=\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{{}\ChorDef{}}l@{}}W.f\hfil{}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}&F\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}W\\
W.x\hfil{}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}&X\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}W\\
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.\mathit{res}\hfil{}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}&W.(f~x)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\end{array}}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{W.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}``done"}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}.(\mathsf{onFinish}~())\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.\mathit{res}}\end{array}}}}

The above function does not actually select a worker;
that job falls to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}
, which maintains a pool of permanent workers, and selects an available worker dynamically to process each request.
However, if all workers are busy,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}
will spawn an ephemeral worker using
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
that terminates after a single job.
The
\mathsf{handleRequest}
function below implements this functionality.

\displaystyle\mathsf{handleRequest}~F~X=\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}acquireWorker}()\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}\cup\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}pool})~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}some}({w})\Rightarrow\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{~}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}&{W}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{w}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}&{\mathsf{runWithWorker}~W~F~X~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}.(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lambda}\_\ldotp\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}releaseWorker}~w)}\end{array}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}none}\Rightarrow\begin{array}[t]{@{}r@{~}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}&{W}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}&{\mathsf{runWithWorker}~W~F~X~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}.(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lambda}\_\ldotp())}\end{array}\\
\end{array}

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}
uses
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}acquireWorker}
, which searches for a free worker and returns
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}some}({w})
if it finds a free worker
w
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}none}
otherwise.
After alerting all relevant parties to the result, the choreography branches.
If the job is run on a free worker,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}M}
releases that worker afterward.
If not, there is nothing to do afterward, as the newly-spawned process falls out of scope and automatically terminates.

Note that this example critically relies on the ability, inherited from
\lambda
qc

(
Samuelson et al. 2025
)
,
to send and receive first-class location name representations and reify them into type-level location names.
Both the output of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}acquireWorker}
and the spawned location name are sent as messages,
and the final worker identity is bound to a type-level location.

4.2.
Operational Semantics

The operational semantics of
\lambda

\pitchfork

consists of a small-step relation using a labeled-transition system
of the form
\langle C_{1},\Omega_{1}\rangle\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}\langle C_{2},\Omega_{2}\rangle
.
The label
R
represents a redex that tracks the specific reduction occurring.
The parameters
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
track the locations who are executing the choreography
before and after the step, respectively, and are used to track which locations remain alive.

Choreographies describe concurrent computation, so the operational semantics includes
out-of-order
reductions
to reflect the ability of locations to execute independently.
These steps allow unrelated actions to occur in different orders, so long as the
order of operations for each location is respected.
Specifically, a step may only be reordered when any computations it is jumping ahead of involve a disjoint set of locations.

\begin{array}[]{@{} >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}c >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l @{}}\textrm{Redices}&R&\Coloneqq&{\rho}.({e_{1}}\rightarrow{e_{2}})\,\mid\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}App}}_{\rho}\,\mid\,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}CaseInl}}_{\rho}\,\mid\,{L}.{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\,\mid\,{L}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}({L^{\prime}},{C})\end{array}

\small\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l}\framebox{$\operatorname{rloc}({R})$}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\framebox{$\operatorname{cloc}({C})$}\\[3.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{0.5em}}c@{\hspace{0.5em}}l}\operatorname{rloc}({{\rho}.({e_{1}}\rightarrow{e_{2}})})\hskip 4.62497pt&\triangleq\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\rho\\
\operatorname{rloc}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}App}}_{\rho}})\hskip 4.62497pt&\triangleq\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\rho\\
\operatorname{rloc}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}CaseInl}}_{\rho}})\hskip 4.62497pt&\triangleq\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\rho\\
\operatorname{rloc}({{L}.{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}})\hskip 4.62497pt&\triangleq\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\{L\}\cup\rho\\
\operatorname{rloc}({{L}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}({L^{\prime}},{C})})\hskip 4.62497pt&\triangleq\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\{L\}\end{array}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\begin{array}[]{r@{\hspace{0.5em}}c@{\hspace{0.5em}}l}\operatorname{cloc}({X})=\operatorname{cloc}({\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fun}}}_{\rho}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}})\hskip 4.62497pt&\triangleq\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\varnothing\\
\operatorname{cloc}({\rho.e})\hskip 4.62497pt&\triangleq\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\rho\\
\operatorname{cloc}({C_{1}\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}C_{2}})\hskip 4.62497pt&\triangleq\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\operatorname{cloc}({C_{1}})\cup\operatorname{cloc}({C_{2}})\cup\rho\\
\operatorname{cloc}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}}})\hskip 4.62497pt&\triangleq\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\operatorname{cloc}({C_{1}})\cup(\operatorname{cloc}({C_{2}})\setminus\{\alpha\})\cup\rho\\
\operatorname{cloc}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\ell}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}})\hskip 4.62497pt&\triangleq\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\{\ell\}\cup(\operatorname{cloc}({C})\setminus\{\alpha\})\\
\operatorname{cloc}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C}})\hskip 4.62497pt&\triangleq\hfil\hskip 4.62497pt&\{L\}\cup\operatorname{cloc}({C})\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 3.

Selected Redices and Location Function Rules.
Here
m
is either a local value
v
or a selection label
d
.

We compute the locations involved in a step using the
redex locations
function
\operatorname{rloc}({R})
,
and the locations (possibly) involved in an entire choreography using the
choreography locations
function
\operatorname{cloc}({C})
.
For example, the redex
{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}.{v}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}
denotes that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
sends
v
to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
.
Since precisely
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
participate in this step,
\operatorname{rloc}({{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}.{v}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}
.
Redices
{\rho}.({e_{1}}\rightarrow{e_{2}})
,
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}App}}_{\rho}
, and
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}CaseInl}}_{\rho}
,
which label stepping a local program
e_{1}
to
e_{2}
,
\beta
-reduction, and branching left, respectively,
all require all locations in
\rho
to participate, so
\operatorname{rloc}({R})=\rho
.
Redex
{L}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}({L^{\prime}},{C})
appears when location
L
spawns a new thread
L^{\prime}
with task
C
.
Since
L^{\prime}
is not alive until after the step occurs, only
L
participates in the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
step.

The function
\operatorname{cloc}({C})
, on the other hand, captures
not only the participants of the next step
C
can make, but
all locations that may eventually participate in a step made by
C
,
Thus, for instance,

\operatorname{cloc}({\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.2}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(2\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}x)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B})}})=\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\},

even though
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
must take multiple steps before
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
gets involved.
Figure
3
shows selected redices and definitions for both location functions.

These two functions together determine when it is safe to reorder steps.
Specifically, a step
R
can execute before an entire computation
C

if the set of participants in the two are disjoint—
\operatorname{cloc}({C})\cap\operatorname{rloc}({R})=\varnothing
—even if a standard in-order semantics would execute
C
to completion before
R
.
The following out-of-order rule for
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}
-expressions is an example of such a step.

\small\frac{\mathchoice{\left\langle{C_{2}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{C_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{C_{2}^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C_{2}^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C_{2}^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C_{2}^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{cloc}({C_{1}})\cap\operatorname{rloc}({R})=\varnothing\hskip 9.24994pt\rho\cap\operatorname{rloc}({R})=\varnothing\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing}{\mathchoice{\left\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}}},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}^{\prime}}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}^{\prime}}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}^{\prime}}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}^{\prime}}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={\footnotesize[C-LetI]},vcenter}

This rule also prohibits the out-of-order step
R
in the body from including locations binding a variable in the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}
,
and ensures that all locations binding the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}
have been resolved by requiring
\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing
.
The latter requirement prevents a situation where a location variable later resolves to a location appearing in the step,
meaning
C-LetI
(
??
) would have rearranged their operations.

Out-of-order execution can similarly occur in branches of
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}case}}
- and
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}
-expressions before fully evaluating the scrutinee,
with some extra requirements on the steps.
Specifically, stepping in the branches is safe when both
(1) the locations involved in the step are disjoint from those computing the scrutinee (similarly to
C-LetI
(
??
)),
and (2) the step will occur regardless of the branch taken.
The latter point is enforced by requiring identical redices and updates to the set of executing locations in the step in both branches.
The result is the following
C-LocalCaseI
(
??
) rule, with an analogous rule for choreographic case expressions.

\small\frac{\mathchoice{\left\langle{C_{1}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{C_{1}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C_{1}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C_{1}},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{C_{1}^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C_{1}^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C_{1}^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C_{1}^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathchoice{\left\langle{C_{2}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{C_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{C_{2}^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C_{2}^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C_{2}^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C_{2}^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{cloc}({C})\cap\operatorname{rloc}({R})=\varnothing\hskip 9.24994pt\rho\cap\operatorname{rloc}({R})=\varnothing\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing}{\mathchoice{\left\langle{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{C_{1}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}}\end{array}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{C_{1}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}}\end{array}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{C_{1}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}}\end{array}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{C_{1}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}}\end{array}},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{C_{1}^{\prime}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}^{\prime}}\end{array}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{C_{1}^{\prime}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}^{\prime}}\end{array}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{C_{1}^{\prime}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}^{\prime}}\end{array}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{C_{1}^{\prime}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}^{\prime}}\end{array}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={\footnotesize[C-LocalCaseI]},vcenter}

To see this rule in action, consider the following out-of-order step.

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}~{\big(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\big)}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}x)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.(3\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2)}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.(3\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2)}\end{array}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow_{c}}{}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}~{\big(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\big)}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}x)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.5}\\
{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.5}\end{array}

Although the scrutinee
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.e\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
is not yet evaluated,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
will run the same program
3\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2
on either branch,
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
is neither involved in computing the scrutinee nor will their control flow branch.
It is thus safe to reduce
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.(3\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2)
to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}.5
in both branches.
In contrast, no out-of-order step is yet available in the following choreography.
