\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}if}}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}~{\big(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.6\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(x\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}<}}3)}\big)}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}then}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(3\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2)}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}else}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.(3\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2)}

Although
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
executes identical expressions in both branches and will not branch,
executing the computation in the branches before the send
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.6\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
in the condition would reorder
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
’s operations, which is disallowed.

\frac{e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e_{2}\quad\quad\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\subseteq\Omega\quad\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing}{\mathchoice{\left\langle{\rho.e_{1}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{\rho.e_{1}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\rho.e_{1}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\rho.e_{1}},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{\rho}.({e_{1}}\rightarrow{e_{2}})}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{\rho.e_{2}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{\rho.e_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\rho.e_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\rho.e_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[C-Done]},vcenter}\frac{f=\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fun}}}_{\rho}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\quad\operatorname{Val}({V})\quad\quad\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\subseteq\Omega\quad\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing}{\mathchoice{\left\langle{f\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}V},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{f\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}V},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{f\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}V},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{f\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}V},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}App}}_{\rho}}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{\mathchoice{C{\left[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V\right]}}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{\mathchoice{C{\left[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V\right]}}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\mathchoice{C{\left[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V\right]}}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\mathchoice{C{\left[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V\right]}}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}},{\Omega}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[C-App]},vcenter}\frac{\operatorname{Val}({v})\quad L_{1}\in\rho_{1}\quad 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}L_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{L_{1}}.{v}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho_{2}}}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}).v},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}).v},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}).v},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}).v},{\Omega}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[C-SendV]},vcenter}\frac{L^{\prime}\notin\Omega\quad\operatorname{fv}({C^{\prime}})=\varnothing\quad L\in\Omega\quad\quad C^{\prime}=\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L^{\prime},\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L^{\prime},\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L^{\prime},\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L^{\prime},\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}]}}{{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{L}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{L}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{L}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{L}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}},{\Omega}\rangle}\\
\quad\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{L}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}({L^{\prime}},{C^{\prime}})}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L^{\prime}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C^{\prime}}},{\Omega\cup\{L^{\prime}\}}\right\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L^{\prime}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C^{\prime}}},{\Omega\cup\{L^{\prime}\}}\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L^{\prime}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C^{\prime}}},{\Omega\cup\{L^{\prime}\}}\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L^{\prime}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C^{\prime}}},{\Omega\cup\{L^{\prime}\}}\rangle}\end{array}}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[C-Fork]},vcenter}\frac{\operatorname{Val}({V})\quad L\in\Omega}{\mathchoice{\left\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{V}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{V}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{V}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{V}},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}({L})}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{V},{\Omega\setminus\{L\}}\right\rangle}{\langle{V},{\Omega\setminus\{L\}}\rangle}{\langle{V},{\Omega\setminus\{L\}}\rangle}{\langle{V},{\Omega\setminus\{L\}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[C-Kill]},vcenter}

Figure 4.
Selected
\lambda

\pitchfork

Operational Semantics

Figure
4
contains a selection of additional rules.
The remaining rules are either very similar to those presented here,
or are nearly identical to ones for the corresponding construct in standard call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus.
The complete semantics can be found in Appendix
A.8
.

C-Done
(
??
) lifts the local-language semantics to choreographies,

C-App
(
??
) applies a function to its argument,
and
C-SendV
(
??
) formalizes the multiply-located semantics of message-passing.
These steps all require that all of the “named locations” in
\rho
(written
\operatorname{NL}({\rho})
) are running, formalized as
\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\subseteq\Omega
.
Thus, every process which needs to perform the action is able to do so.
Formally, the named locations function
\operatorname{NL}({\rho})
homomorphically collects all concrete location names appearing in the set.
Its full definition can be found in Appendix
D.4
.

The final two rules formalize how
\lambda

\pitchfork

spawns and kills new locations.
To spawn a location,
C-Fork
(
??
) selects a globally fresh location name
L^{\prime}

and binds
\alpha
to
L^{\prime}
and
x
to its representation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}
in the body of the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expression.
It checks that the substituted body
C^{\prime}
is closed to ensure that
L^{\prime}
—which has no access to any enclosing scope—does not attempt to execute a program with free variables.
Finally, it wraps
C^{\prime}
in a
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
term to denote that
L^{\prime}
should be killed after the computation completes
and adds
L^{\prime}
to the set
\Omega
of executing locations.
Once the computation completes,
C-Kill
(
??
) kills the spawned location by removing it from
\Omega
and returning the output of the computation.
There is also an out-of-order version of
C-Kill
(
??
) that allows a spawned thread
L
to terminate
when its part of the inner computation
C
is complete; that is, when
L\notin\operatorname{cloc}({C})
.

Example 3 (Fork Bomb).

Note that this semantics supports programs that spawn an unbounded number of locations.
For example, the following
\mathsf{forkBomb}
program never terminates, and generates an exponentially large number of spawned threads as it runs.

\mathsf{forkBomb}~=~\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}tfun}}}{F}({\ell\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{{}\ChorDef{}}l@{}}\alpha\hfil{}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}&\ell.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()\\
\beta\hfil{}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}&\ell.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()\end{array}}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{F\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\alpha}}\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}F\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\beta}}\beta}\end{array}}}}

4.3.
Static Semantics

The static semantics of our language is defined by a kinding judgment and a typing judgment.

4.3.1.
Kinding System

\begin{array}[]{@{} >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}c >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l @{}}\textrm{Kinds}&\kappa&\Coloneqq&*_{\mathsf{loc}}\,\mid\,*_{\mathsf{locset}}\,\mid\,*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}\,\mid\,*_{e}\,\mid\,*_{\rho}\\
\textrm{Local Program Types}&t_{e}&\Coloneqq&\alpha\,\mid\,\mathsf{int}\,\mid\,\mathsf{bool}\,\mid\,\mathsf{loc}_{\rho}\,\mid\,\mathsf{locset}_{\rho}\,\mid\,\ldots\\
\textrm{Locations}&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\ldots&\in&\mathcal{L}\\
\textrm{Choreography Types}&\ell,\rho,\tau,t&\Coloneqq&\alpha\,\mid\,t_{e}@\rho\,\mid\,\tau_{1}\xrightarrow{\rho}\tau_{2}\,\mid\,\forall\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa[\rho]\ldotp\tau\\
&&\,\mid&\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}\,\mid\,\tau_{1}+_{\rho}\tau_{2}\,\mid\,\mu_{\rho}\alpha\ldotp\tau\\
&&\,\mid&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}\,\mid\,\{\ell\}\,\mid\,\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}\,\mid\,\top\end{array}

Figure 5.
Syntax of Types and Kinds. Here
\alpha
is a type variable.

To support type and process polymorphism, we define a kinding judgment
\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}t\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}\kappa
, where
\Gamma
is a kinding context,

t
is a type, and
\kappa
is a kind.
The kind
\kappa
classifies
t
as either a location (
*_{\mathsf{loc}}
),
a set of locations (
*_{\mathsf{locset}}
),
a finite set of locations (
*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}
),
a local program type (
*_{e}
), or a choreographic program type (
*_{\rho}
).
Figure
5
presents the syntax for these types and kinds.

Types of kind
*_{e}
are precisely the types included in the local language under a given type variable context.
The kind
*_{\mathsf{loc}}
represents location names, which can refer to either concrete locations
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}\in\mathcal{L}
or in-context location variables,
while the kind
*_{\mathsf{locset}}
classifies (non-empty) sets of location names, which can be either a type variable,
a singleton set (
\{\ell\}
), a union of sets (
\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}
),
or the expression
\top
representing the infinite set containing all locations in
\mathcal{L}
.

The infinite set
\top
is vital to our treatment of process polymorphism, as described below.
However, in some instances location sets cannot contain
\top
(i.e., they are
finite
).
For example, the program
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}1)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\top
would require
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
to send infinitely many messages.
The kind
*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}
of finite location sets formalizes this restriction by excluding
\top
.

To interpret types of kind
*_{\mathsf{locset}}
and
*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}
as sets, we define the containment
\ell\in\rho
relation, the subset
\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho_{2}
relation,
and the set difference function
\rho_{1}\setminus\rho_{2}
by syntactic recursion.
These operations satisfy many of the expected properties from set theory (e.g., the subset relation is a preorder),
as well as additional properties governing their behavior with respect to type substitution.
As
\top
represents all locations,
\ell\in\top
for all
\ell
, including unresolved type variables.
The formal definition of these operations can be found in Appendix
A.7
.

The kind
*_{\rho}
of program types is similar to the standard program type
*
of System F and
\lambda
qc
,
but includes a location set parameter
\rho
bounding who must know about any value with a type of that kind.
For instance, the type
\mathsf{int}@\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}
—an integer located at both
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
—has kind
*_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}
,
as any value
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.n
of this type is known to
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
, but nobody else.
For a compound type
\tau
of kind
*_{\rho}
, every location who knows any part of a value of type
\tau
must appear in
\rho
.
The
K-Prod
(
??
) and
K-Sum
(
??
) rules give two examples of this principle.

\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{1}}\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{2}}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-Prod]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{1}}\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{2}}\quad\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}\quad\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}\subseteq\rho}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}+_{\rho}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-Sum]},vcenter}

In
K-Prod
(
??
), if a location knows (part of) either side of a pair, then they know part of the entire pair.

One may expect
K-Sum
(
??
) to follow a similar rule: collect the annotations on each side.
However, sums carry information beyond the underlying types; they also convey if the value is an
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inl}}
or an
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inr}}
.
The
\rho
on the plus describes
who knows which side the value is on
, which may include more people than know the data on each side.
For instance, a value of type
(\mathsf{int}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}+_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}}\mathsf{int}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}}

could be an
\mathsf{int}
at either
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
or
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
, but all of
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
,
and

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
know which.
Requiring
\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}\subseteq\rho
ensures that everyone who might hold data knows whether or not they need to hold that data.

A function’s existence and use must be known to anyone aware of its inputs, aware of its outputs, or involved in computing its body—the
latent participants
.
To track this, we augment function types with a set
\rho
describing the latent participants.
The kinding rule
K-Arrow
(
??
) gives a function type a kind including all three sets of locations in its bound.

A forall type
\forall{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}[{\rho}]\ldotp{\tau}
similarly tracks latent participants with the annotation
\rho
.
Just as the output type of a type function can depend on its argument, so too can its participants.
Thus in forall types abstracting over a location (set),
\alpha
is bound in both
\tau

and
\rho
.
When abstracting over other kinds, however,
\alpha
is not bound in
\rho
, as the participants are fixed.
Recall from Section
4.1
that all locations must know the definition of every process-polymorphic type function declared in their lifetime.
Such a forall type must therefore have the kind
*_{\top}
, as reflected by
K-AllLoc
(
??
).

\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}\quad\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{1}}\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{2}}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}\xrightarrow{\rho}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}\cup\rho}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-Arrow]},vcenter}\frac{\kappa\in\{*_{\mathsf{loc}},*_{\mathsf{locset}},*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}\}\quad\quad\Gamma,\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{\tau}}\quad\Gamma,\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}\quad}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\forall{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}[{\rho}]\ldotp{\tau}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\top}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-AllLoc]},vcenter}

4.3.2.
Type System

Typing judgments in
\lambda

\pitchfork

take the form
\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho
,
where
\Theta=\Gamma;\Delta_{e};\Delta
is a three-part context of type, local, and choreographic variables,

C
is a choreography,
\tau
is a program type, and
\rho
is a set of participants who may be involved in computing
C
.

Participant Tracking

Just as the participant parameter
\rho
on the kind
*_{\rho}
bounds the types of that kind,
the participant parameter
\rho
in the typing judgment bounds the locations which might participate in choreography
C
.

This information is used to ensure that a thread, once killed, will not be asked to perform further computation.
To see the challenge in enforcing this guarantee,
consider the following program:

\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{F}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\left(\begin{array}[c]{@{\,}r@{~}l@{\,}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}&\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}&({\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\lambda}\_\ldotp\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{(\alpha.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A})}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x})\end{array}\right)}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{F~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.()}

Here
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
spawns a thread
\alpha
who then immediately dies,
as the body of the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expression—the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\lambda}
-abstraction—is a value.
However, the returned abstraction closes over
\alpha
and, if applied,
would send a message to
\alpha
—which is now dead—causing deadlock.

The
\lambda

\pitchfork

type system has two key features to prevent this scenario.
First, it uses
\rho
to track which locations might participate in a choreography.
For instance, the body of the
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\lambda}
-abstraction types as

\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{(\alpha.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A})}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\mathsf{int}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\{\alpha,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\}.

indicating that
\alpha
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
might participate in the function body, but nobody else will.

Second, as mentioned in Section
4.3.1
, we augment function types to include the set of latent participants.
Here, for instance, the full
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\lambda}
-abstraction is typed as

\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}({\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\lambda}\_\ldotp\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{(\alpha.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A})}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x})\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\mathsf{unit}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\xrightarrow{\smash{\{\alpha,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\}}}\mathsf{int}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\varnothing

with latent participants
\{\alpha,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\}
located above the function arrow.
Note that
\rho=\varnothing
here since an abstraction is a value so no locations are involved in computing it.
Crucially, because type variable
\alpha
representing a spawned thread is free in the function type,
we should rule out the enclosing
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expression
to prevent computation involving
\alpha
from escaping the scope of its lifetime.
Indeed, our formal typing rule defined below employs this exact logic.

By contrast, the program below is valid, since the
type
\mathsf{unit}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\xrightarrow{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}}\mathsf{int}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
of the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
’s body does not contain
\alpha
,
indicating that it is safe to use the value after
\alpha
is killed.

\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{F}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\left(\begin{array}[c]{@{\,}r@{~}l@{\,}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}l@{}}\alpha&{}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\\
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.y&{}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}\alpha.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2)\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\end{array}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}&({\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\lambda}\_\ldotp\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}.y\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A})}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x})\end{array}\right)}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{F~\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.()}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\mathsf{int}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}

Typing Rules

\frac{\Theta,F\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mu\tau_{1}\xrightarrow{\rho}\tau_{2},X\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mu\tau_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho\quad\quad\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{a}}\quad\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{b}}\quad\quad\rho^{\prime}=\rho_{a}\cup\rho_{b}\cup\rho}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fun}}}_{\rho^{\prime}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau_{1}\xrightarrow{\rho}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\varnothing}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[T-Fun]},vcenter}\frac{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau_{1}\xrightarrow{\rho}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho_{1}\quad\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho_{2}\quad\quad\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{a}}\quad\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{b}}\quad\quad\rho^{\prime}=\rho_{a}\cup\rho_{b}\cup\rho}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C_{1}\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho^{\prime}}}C_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}\cup\rho^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[T-App]},vcenter}\frac{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}t_{e}@\rho_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho\quad\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho_{2}\quad\quad\Theta,\rho_{1}.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho^{\prime}}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho_{1}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho\cup\rho^{\prime}\cup\rho_{1}}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[T-LetLocal]},vcenter}\frac{\Theta,\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}},\{\ell,\alpha\}.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mu\mathsf{loc}_{\alpha}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho\quad\quad\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\ell\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{loc}}\quad\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{\tau}}}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\ell}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\{\ell\}\cup(\rho\setminus\{\alpha\})}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[T-Fork]},vcenter}\frac{\kappa\in\{*_{\mathsf{loc}},*_{\mathsf{locset}},*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}\}\quad\quad\Theta,F\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mu\forall\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa[\rho]\ldotp\tau,\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\forall\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa[\rho]\ldotp\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\varnothing}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[T-TFunLoc]},vcenter}\frac{\kappa\in\{*_{\mathsf{loc}},*_{\mathsf{locset}},*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}\}\quad\quad\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\forall\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa[\rho]\ldotp\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho_{1}\quad\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}t\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}\kappa\quad\quad\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\mathchoice{\tau{\left[\alpha\mapsto t\right]}}{\tau[\alpha\mapsto t]}{\tau[\alpha\mapsto t]}{\tau[\alpha\mapsto t]}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{\tau}}\quad\rho^{\prime}=\rho_{\tau}\cup\mathchoice{\rho{\left[\alpha\mapsto t\right]}}{\rho[\alpha\mapsto t]}{\rho[\alpha\mapsto t]}{\rho[\alpha\mapsto t]}}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho^{\prime}}}t\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\mathchoice{\tau{\left[\alpha\mapsto t\right]}}{\tau[\alpha\mapsto t]}{\tau[\alpha\mapsto t]}{\tau[\alpha\mapsto t]}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho_{1}\cup\rho^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[T-TAppLoc]},vcenter}

Figure 6.
Selected
\lambda

\pitchfork

Typing Rules

Figure
6
presents a selection of
\lambda

\pitchfork

typing rules.
The abstraction and application rules formalize this participant tracking intuition.

T-Fun
(
??
) includes the standard premise for typing a named recursive function—the body must be well-typed with the name and argument in scope.
This rule also facilitates participant tracking by enforcing
that the latent participants
\rho
on the function type must be the same as the participants in the body of the function.
Finally, the rule ensures that all latent participants along with anyone who might know the input or output must know the function’s definition.

T-App
(
??
) includes standard premises checking that the function and argument are well-typed and match.
Similarly to
T-Fun
(
??
), it requires every location involved in the input, output, or body of the function to perform the application.
Lastly, the locations involved in the entire expression include anyone involved in computing
C_{1}
or
C_{2}

as well as anyone who performs the application.

T-LetLocal
(
??
) types
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}
-expressions by ensuring the
computation in the head of the expression produces a multiply-located value at all locations in
\rho_{2}
.
A selected subset
\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho_{2}
can then bind local variable
x
to this value in the body,
whose type is given to the entire expression.
The participants of the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}
are then collected from the head, body, and anyone who binds the variable.

The new
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expression is typed by
T-Fork
(
??
).
The first two premises are straightforward:
the body of the expression must be well-typed with the new location variable
\alpha
and
its first-class representation
x
in scope,
and the parent location
\ell
must be well-kinded as a location.

The third requirement—that the type
\tau
of the body is well-kinded
without
\alpha
in scope
—serves two purposes.
First, it prevents type dependency.
As the name of the spawned thread is chosen at runtime, we cannot know a-priori which name
\alpha
will resolve to,
so we cannot assign a coherent type to the overall
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expression if that type may depend on the thread’s name.
Second, it prevents spawned threads from being asked to perform computation after they are killed.
Because our type system tracks latent participants,
the kinding judgement ensures that the type does not refer to any out-of-scope locations

even in pending computations inside (type) functions
.

Like a
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}
-expression, the type of a
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expression is given by the type of its body.
Its participants must include any participant in the body (
\rho
), as well as the parent
\ell
.
However, even if they participate in the body,
the spawned thread
\alpha
is not considered a participant of the whole expression,
as any surrounding scope need not know about them.
The participants are thus only
\{\ell\}\cup(\rho\setminus\{\alpha\})
.
Identically to the restriction on
\tau
, the spawned thread
\alpha
is removed from
\rho
primarily to avoid type dependency.
Excluding
\alpha
also has a secondary effect of preventing the type system from tracking whether an as-yet-unspawned thread might participate in a future computation.
However, the participant set still soundly tracks whether a currently-spawned location might perform computation in the future,
which we formalize in Section
4.3.3
below.

Note that there is no typing rule for
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
expressions.
The type system is only intended to handle surface-language programs,
so we explicitly exclude
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
.
In Section
4.3.3
we introduce an augmented type system which can handle
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
,
allowing us to prove type soundness.

T-TFunLoc
(
??
) and
T-TAppLoc
(
??
) handle process polymorphic type functions and applications.
Just like for standard functions,
T-TFunLoc
(
??
) requires the latent participants in the forall type
to match the participants in the body of a process-polymorphic type function.
Recall that all locations are required to know the definition of such a function.

The type application rule
T-TAppLoc
(
??
) is similar to the standard application rule,
but since
\alpha
may be free in type
\tau
and latent participants
\rho
, we substitute it for the now-resolved variable.
This means that although everyone must know the function definition,
only those locations who know the output type or are involved in the function body
after resolving
\alpha
must perform the application.

The remaining typing rules can be found in Appendix
B
.

Example 4 (Parallel Divide-and-Conquer).

To see how participant tracking and polymorphism work in-tandem,
consider the following parallel divide-and-conquer algorithm for list summation.

\displaystyle\mathsf{recursiveSum}:\forall{\ell\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}}}[{\{\ell\}}]\ldotp{\mathsf{list}({\mathsf{int}})@\ell\xrightarrow{\smash{\{\ell\}}}\mathsf{int}@\ell}

\displaystyle\mathsf{recursiveSum}~\ell~\mathit{XS}~=~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}if}}~\ell.(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}len}~\mathit{XS}\mathrel{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}<}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}LOCAL\_MAX\_LEN})\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}then}}~\ell.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}sum}(\mathit{XS})\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}else}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}(\ell.\mathit{xs}_{1},\ell.\mathit{xs}_{2})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}\ell.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}split}(\mathit{XS})\\
\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}\ell.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()\\
\alpha.\mathit{xs}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}\ell.\mathit{xs}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\alpha\\
\alpha.s_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}\mathsf{recursiveSum}\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\alpha}}\alpha\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\alpha}}\alpha.\mathit{xs}_{1}\\
\ell.s_{2}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}\mathsf{recursiveSum}\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\ell}}\ell\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\ell}}\ell.\mathit{xs}_{2}\\
\ell.s_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}\alpha.s_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\ell\\
\end{array}\\
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~\ell.(s_{1}\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}s_{2})\end{array}\end{array}

This choreography finds the sum of the list
\mathit{XS}
owned by
\ell
.
The first line checks if
\mathit{XS}
is long enough to be worth parallelizing; otherwise,
\ell
sums the list locally.
For longer lengths,
\ell
splits
\mathit{XS}
into two halves,
\mathit{xs}_{1}
and
\mathit{xs}_{2}
, recursively summing each half in parallel.
To perform this parallelism,
\ell
then spawns
\alpha
, and sends it the first half of
\mathit{XS}
.
The new thread
\alpha
then calls
\mathsf{recursiveSum}
using this value, potentially spawning its own children to sum its half of the list.
After both processes have summed their halves,
\alpha
sends its sum
s_{1}
to
\ell
.
At this point, the thread
\alpha
falls out of scope and (implicitly) dies.
Finally,
\ell
returns the sum
s_{1}\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}s_{2}
.

4.3.3.
Type Soundness

The type system described above enjoys two important notions of soundness:
the parameter
\rho
in the typing judgment captures all locations who may take a step,
and the standard guarantee that a well-typed choreography does not get stuck.

The following theorem formalizes the first notion.
Recall from Section
4.2
that each step includes a redex
R
and
\operatorname{rloc}({R})
computes the set of locations involved in
R
.

Theorem 1 (Sound Participants).

If
\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho
and
\mathchoice{\left\langle{C},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\overset{\smash{R}\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}
,
then
\operatorname{rloc}({R})\subseteq\rho
.

Note that the soundness of
\rho
does not directly extend to multiple steps:
if the step spawns a thread, then the participants in the choreography may increase.
However, this theorem can be combined with type preservation (Theorem
3
below)
to ensure that
C^{\prime}
will be typed at
some
set
\rho^{\prime}
.
Moreover, the locations that materialize in
\rho^{\prime}
are precisely those locations spawned by the step
R
(i.e., in
\Omega^{\prime}\setminus\Omega
),
meaning
\rho
tracks all future steps made by
currently-spawned
locations.

Our traditional type soundness results follows from a standard progress-and-preservation argument.
However, types are not preserved in the surface type system above, as stepping
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
immediately creates a
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
term.
Not only is there no typing rule for
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
,
but adding a naïve one is unsound:
the typing rule can only examine the
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
’s body,
and cannot prevent any
external scopes
from referencing the thread after it dies.
For instance, the hypothetically well-typed program
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.1})}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}

will encounter a deadlock due to a live process (
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
) attempting to contact a dead one (
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
) in the send operation
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.x\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
.

We therefore introduce an augmented typing judgment
\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\scriptscriptstyle+}}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho
that soundly handles programs containing
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
by making all enclosing scopes responsible for ensuring spawned threads are not referenced outside of their lifetimes.
Its typing rules are based on the surface rules, but add a few extra premises.
For example, the augmented rule
S-LetLocal
(
??
) shown below types
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}
-expressions.

\frac{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\scriptscriptstyle+}}C_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}t_{e}@\rho_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho\quad\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho_{2}\quad\quad\Theta,\rho_{1}.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\scriptscriptstyle+}}C_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho^{\prime}\quad\quad\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\cap\operatorname{SL}({C_{2}})=\varnothing\quad\operatorname{NL}({\rho^{\prime}})\cap\operatorname{SL}({C_{1}})=\varnothing\quad\quad\operatorname{NL}({\rho_{1}})\cap(\operatorname{SL}({C_{1}})\cup\operatorname{SL}({C_{2}}))=\varnothing}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\scriptscriptstyle+}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho_{1}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C_{1}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C_{2}}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho\cup\rho^{\prime}\cup\rho_{1}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[S-LetLocal]},vcenter}\frac{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\scriptscriptstyle+}}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho\quad L\notin\operatorname{SL}({C})}{\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\scriptscriptstyle+}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho\cup\{L\}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[S-Kill]},vcenter}

The first three premises of
S-LetLocal
(
??
) are identical to
T-LetLocal
(
??
) (replacing
\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}
with
\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\scriptscriptstyle+}}
),
and the last three premises enforce that a spawned thread will not be expected to perform any computation outside of its lexical scope.
This well-scoping is enforced by using the participant tracking information to determine who will perform computation in a given subexpression,
and the spawned-locations function
\operatorname{SL}({C})
to compute the lexical scope of each thread.
The function
\operatorname{SL}({C})
is homomorphically defined to collect all locations
L
that appear in a subterm
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C^{\prime}}
of
C
.
In a
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}
-expression, there are three ill-scoping possibilities to rule-out:
a thread spawned in the head performs computation in the body,
a thread spawned in the body performs computation in the head,
and a thread spawned in the head or body is expected to bind the variable.
The typing rule prevents all three possibilities.

The other rules of this judgment share an identical goal of ensuring a spawned thread will never perform an action outside its lexical scope.
For instance,
S-Kill
(
??
) above forbids situations where the spawned thread
L
in a
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
appears in a different
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
expression in its body.
The other rules of the augmented typing judgment can be found in Appendix
B.4
.

This augmented typing judgment is fully sound with respect to the operational semantics,
as demonstrated by the progress and preservation theorems below.

Theorem 2 (Progress).

If
\,\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\scriptscriptstyle+}}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho
and
\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\subseteq\Omega
,
then either
C
is a value or
\mathchoice{\left\langle{C},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}
can step.

Theorem 3 (Preservation).

If
\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\scriptscriptstyle+}}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho
,

\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\subseteq\Omega
, and

\langle C,\Omega\rangle\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{c}}\langle C^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime}\rangle
,
then there is some
\rho^{\prime}
such that

\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\scriptscriptstyle+}}C^{\prime}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho^{\prime}
,

\operatorname{NL}({\rho^{\prime}})\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
, and

\operatorname{NL}({\rho^{\prime}})\setminus\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}\setminus\Omega
.

The additional premise to the theorems that
\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\subseteq\Omega

simply means that all locations who might participate in the choreography must be running.
The use of
\operatorname{NL}({\rho})
is a technical requirement to handle when
\rho
contains
\top
.
The penultimate conclusion of the preservation theorem relates the new participant set
\rho^{\prime}
that types
C^{\prime}

with the new set
\Omega^{\prime}
of running processes, ensuring the extra premise is maintained.
The last conclusion of preservation ensures that an unexpected process does not materialize in
\rho^{\prime}
,
allowing participant soundness (Theorem
1
) to be extended to multiple steps.

Finally, for choreographies without
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
,
the two type systems are equivalent,
yielding soundness of the standard type system when combined with progress and preservation.

Theorem 4 (Typing Equivalence).

\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho
if and only if
\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash^{\mkern-3.0mu\scriptscriptstyle+}}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho
and
C
has no
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
.

5.
Network Language

To compile a choreography into multiple programs that a system can execute concurrently,
we need to specify the target language that the system will run.
This
network language
proscribes the actions of each individual location
in the system, and gives a concurrent operational semantics to describe the execution of the entire system.

5.1.
Network Language Syntax

\begin{array}[]{@{} >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}c >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l @{}}\textrm{Network Program}&E&\Coloneqq&X\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e})\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{E}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\ell}\\
&&\,\mid&E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{2}\,\mid\,\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E}\,\mid\,E_{1}~E_{2}\,\mid\,\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E}\,\mid\,E~t\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2}}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2}}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fold}}~{E}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}unfold}}~{E}\,\mid\,(E_{1},E_{2})\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fst}}~{E}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}snd}}~{E}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{E}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{E}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}case}}~{E}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}~(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{X}\Rightarrow{E_{1}})~(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{Y}\Rightarrow{E_{2}})\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}localCase}}~{E}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{E_{1}})~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{E_{2}})\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}~({d}\Rightarrow{E})\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}~(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}})~(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}})\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choose}}~{d}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}for}}~{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}{E}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}~{\rho}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2}}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({E_{1}})~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2}}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}\\
\textrm{Systems}&\Pi&\Coloneqq&L_{1}\triangleright E_{1}\mathrel{\parallel}\ldots\mathrel{\parallel}L_{n}\triangleright E_{n}\\
\end{array}

Figure 7.
Selected Network Program Syntax. Here
L\in\mathcal{L}
is a concrete location name.

The network language is a concurrent
\lambda
-calculus with messages from the same space as in choreographies—local language values and selection messages.
The syntax, given in Figure
7
, closely mirrors our choreographic syntax,
except we split message sends—including selection messages—into two separate constructs to account for the
sender and recipient(s).

The return expression
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e})
is used to execute and yield the output of a local expression
e
,
mirroring the choreography
\rho.e
.
To account for a location
L\notin\rho
—who should not execute
e
—and other scenarios
where a location is not involved in part of the overall choreography, we include a unit value
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}
which does nothing.

To model message-passing, we need two separate constructs for the sender and recipient(s) of a message.
Specifically, the send expression
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{E}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}
multicasts the result of program
E
to every location in
\rho

(and also has the sender yield the output of
E
),
and the dual receive expression
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\ell}
waits to receive a local value from
\ell
.
Since only local values can be sent,
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}
may only send a value
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({v})
,
while other forms such as
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}
will be stuck.

(Type) let-expressions, (type) functions,
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}case}}
,
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}localCase}}
, and algebraic and recursive data types are included in
the network language identically to in choreographies, less some un-needed location (set) annotations.
The sequencing construct
E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{2}
is standard.

On top of these relatively familiar expressions, there are a two (groups of) constructions that are standard in (process-polymorphic) choreographies.
Recall that a location can participate in a
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}case}}
or
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}localCase}}
expression if it either knows the data being branched on
or synchronization messages are inserted telling it which way to go
.
In the second case, we need some way to represent that location branching on the result of waiting for a synchronization message.
We do this using
\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow-choice}}}
expressions.
Specifically,
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}~(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}})~(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}})
is the program that waits for a synchronization message from
\ell
.
If it is
\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}
, then it continues as
E_{1}
; otherwise, it continues as
E_{2}
.
Note that if a synchronization message in a choreography is used outside of a branch, then we statically know what message will be received.
In this case, we allow an
\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow-choice}}}
expression to only have one branch.
Such a term receiving the wrong synchronization message becomes stuck.

Next, the “AmI-In” expression, introduced by
\lambda
qc
as a generalization of “AmI” from PolyChor
\lambda

(
Graversen et al. 2024
)
,
intuitively represents a process’s knowledge of its own name.
In particular,
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}~{\rho}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2}}
continues as
E_{1}
if the process running it is in
\rho
, and as
E_{2}
otherwise.
At a technical level, EPP uses
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}
to implement process polymorphism.
For instance, the behavior of a location
L
when executing its part of choreography
\alpha.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2)
depends on whether
\alpha
resolves to a location set containing
L
.

\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}
gives the network language a means to branch based on a location’s identity.

The only novel network-language features in this work are the
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}
and
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}
expressions, which implement process forking.
The network-level
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}
expression
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({E_{1}})~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2}}
spawns a new thread with the network code
E_{1}
, called the
thread task
.
The name of this thread is then bound to
\alpha
while a local representation of this name is bound to
x
, similar to
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expressions in choreographies.
Note that, unlike in a choreography, the thread task is explicitly specified in the term, rather than being implicit from scoping.
Dually, the
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}
command halts execution, removing the location from the system.

5.2.
Network Language Operational Semantics

\begin{array}[]{@{} >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}c >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l @{}}\textrm{Transition Labels}&l&\Coloneqq&\iota\,\mid\,{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\,\mid\,{L}.{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({L},{E})\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}\end{array}

\frac{e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e_{2}}{L\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e_{1}})\mathrel{{\overset{\iota\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e_{2}})}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-Ret]},vcenter}\frac{f=\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E}\quad\operatorname{Val}({V})}{L\triangleright f~V\mathrel{{\overset{\iota\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}}\mathchoice{E{\left[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V\right]}}{E[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{E[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{E[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-App]},vcenter}\frac{\operatorname{Val}({v})\quad\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing}{L\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({v})}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{v}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho\setminus\{L\}}}}{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({v})}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-Send]},vcenter}\frac{\operatorname{Val}({v})\quad L^{\prime}\neq L}{L\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{L^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{L^{\prime}}.{v}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}}}{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({v})}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-Recv]},vcenter}\frac{\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing}{L\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choose}}~{d}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}for}}~{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}{E}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{d}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho\setminus\{L\}}}}{}}E}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-Choose]},vcenter}\frac{L^{\prime}\neq L}{L\triangleright\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{L^{\prime}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\Rightarrow{{E_{2}}_{\bot}}\end{array}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{L^{\prime}}.{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}}}{}}E_{1}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-AllowL]},vcenter}\frac{{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}E_{1}^{\prime}=\mathchoice{E_{1}{\left[\alpha\mapsto L^{\prime},\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}\right]}}{E_{1}[\alpha\mapsto L^{\prime},\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}]}{E_{1}[\alpha\mapsto L^{\prime},\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}]}{E_{1}[\alpha\mapsto L^{\prime},\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}]}\quad E_{2}^{\prime}=\mathchoice{E_{2}{\left[\alpha\mapsto L^{\prime},\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}\right]}}{E_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L^{\prime},\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}]}{E_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L^{\prime},\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}]}{E_{2}[\alpha\mapsto L^{\prime},\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\lceil}L^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\rfloor}]}\end{array}}\quad{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\operatorname{fv}({E_{1}^{\prime}})=\varnothing\end{array}}}{L\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({E_{1}})~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2}}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({L^{\prime}},{E_{1}^{\prime}})}}{}}E_{2}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-Fork]},vcenter}\frac{}{L\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}}}{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-Exit]},vcenter}

Figure 8.
Selected Network Language Operational Semantics

The labeled transition system

L\triangleright E_{1}\mathrel{{\overset{\smash{\scriptstyle l}\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}}E_{2}

gives the operational semantics of the network language,
where
L
is the location executing the program and
l
is the label on the step.
Selected transition labels and rules are shown in Figure
8
.

There are five forms of transition labels, corresponding to five different sorts of steps.
The “iota” label
\iota
denotes an internal step,
in which the network program or a local program reduces without interaction between other locations.

The send
{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}
and receive
{L}.{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}
labels account for message-passing steps, including selection messages.
As recipients cannot know the contents of a message in advance, the rules
N-Recv
(
??
) and
N-AllowL
(
??
) (and the omitted
N-AllowR
rule)
are non-deterministic, and allow any value to arrive.
The sender follows the
N-Send
(
??
) and
N-Choose
(
??
) rules, which ensure that the contents of the transition label match the message and recipients specified by the program.
The system semantics defined below ensures the sender and recipient agree on the message, resolving the recipient’s non-determinism.

The label
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({L},{E})
indicates the spawning of a new thread and includes the name
L
of the thread and its task
E
.
While
N-Fork
(
??
) allows the new thread’s name to be non-deterministically chosen,
the system semantics ensures there is no collision with an existing name.
The dual label
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}
denotes when a thread is killed.
While the rule
N-Exit
(
??
) has no special effect in this single-location semantics,
the corresponding rule in the system semantics will entirely remove the thread from the system.

5.2.1.
Network Systems

\begin{array}[]{@{} >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}c >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l @{}}\textrm{System Transition Labels}&l_{S}&\Coloneqq&\iota_{L}\,\mid\,L_{1}.m\rightsquigarrow\rho\,\mid\,{L}.\mathtt{fork}({L^{\prime}},{E})\,\mid\,\mathtt{kill}({L})\end{array}

\displaystyle\framebox{$\operatorname{loc}({l_{S}})$}

\displaystyle\operatorname{loc}({\iota_{L}})=\operatorname{loc}({{L}.\mathtt{fork}({L^{\prime}},{E})})=\operatorname{loc}({\mathtt{kill}({L})})\triangleq\{L\}

\displaystyle\operatorname{loc}({L_{1}.m\rightsquigarrow\rho})\triangleq\{L_{1}\}\cup\rho

\frac{L\triangleright\Pi(L)\mathrel{{\overset{\iota\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}}E}{\Pi\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\iota_{L}}}{}_{S}}\mathchoice{\Pi{\left[L\mapsto E\right]}}{\Pi[L\mapsto E]}{\Pi[L\mapsto E]}{\Pi[L\mapsto E]}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[Internal]},vcenter}\frac{L_{1}\notin\rho\quad L_{1}\triangleright\Pi(L_{1})\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}}}{}}E_{1}\quad\quad\forall L\in\rho\ldotp\Big(L\triangleright\Pi(L)\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{L_{1}}.{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}}}{}}E_{L}\Big)\quad}{\Pi\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{L_{1}.m\rightsquigarrow\rho}}{}_{S}}\mathchoice{\Pi{\left[L_{1}\mapsto E_{1},\mkern 2.0muL\in\rho\mapsto E_{L}\right]}}{\Pi[L_{1}\mapsto E_{1},\mkern 2.0muL\in\rho\mapsto E_{L}]}{\Pi[L_{1}\mapsto E_{1},\mkern 2.0muL\in\rho\mapsto E_{L}]}{\Pi[L_{1}\mapsto E_{1},\mkern 2.0muL\in\rho\mapsto E_{L}]}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[Comm]},vcenter}\frac{L^{\prime}\notin\operatorname{dom}({\Pi})\quad\operatorname{fv}({E_{1}})=\varnothing\quad L\triangleright\Pi(L)\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({L^{\prime}},{E_{1}})}}{}}E_{2}}{\Pi\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{L}.\mathtt{fork}({L^{\prime}},{E_{1}})}}{}_{S}}\mathchoice{\Pi{\left[L^{\prime}\mapsto(E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}),\mkern 2.0muL\mapsto E_{2}\right]}}{\Pi[L^{\prime}\mapsto(E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}),\mkern 2.0muL\mapsto E_{2}]}{\Pi[L^{\prime}\mapsto(E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}),\mkern 2.0muL\mapsto E_{2}]}{\Pi[L^{\prime}\mapsto(E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}),\mkern 2.0muL\mapsto E_{2}]}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[Fork]},vcenter}\frac{L\triangleright\Pi(L)\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}}}{}}E}{\Pi\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\mathtt{kill}({L})}}{}_{S}}\Pi\setminus L}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[Kill]},vcenter}

Figure 9.
System Semantics and Labels

Since network programs represent the isolated execution of a single program at a given location,
while choreographies represent an entire concurrent system,
we need to lift the semantics of our network programs to model an entire system.
Formally, we represent a
system

\Pi={\parallel_{L\in\Omega}(L\triangleright E_{L})}

as a map from each location
L
in a finite set
\Omega\subset\mathcal{L}
to the network program
E_{L}
it is currently executing.

The operational semantics of systems are shown in Figure
9
.
These rules lift the single-location semantics into a concurrent composition using four rules.
The
Internal
(
??
) rule allows one location to independently take an internal step.

Comm
(
??
) models message passing, and requires the sender and all recipients to simultaneously step with the same message value.

Fork
(
??
) spawns a new child thread with a fresh name
L^{\prime}
, allowing the parent to specify which code the child should run as long as all variables are resolved.
Finally,
Kill
(
??
) kills a thread by removing it from the system.
Notationally,
\mathchoice{\Pi{\left[L\in\rho\mapsto E_{L}\right]}}{\Pi[L\in\rho\mapsto E_{L}]}{\Pi[L\in\rho\mapsto E_{L}]}{\Pi[L\in\rho\mapsto E_{L}]}
denotes the updated system mapping
L
to
E_{L}
if
L\in\rho
and
\Pi(L)
otherwise,
and
\Pi\setminus L
denotes the system which is identical to
\Pi
, but removes
L
from its domain.

6.
Endpoint Projection

With the target language in hand, we now define and prove correct the endpoint projection (EPP) procedure
to compile a choreography into a system of concurrently executing network programs.

6.1.
Projecting One Location

The projection of a choreography
C
for a single location
L
, denoted
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}
, is the network program that executes the actions involving
L
in
C
.
EPP is partial, both because the
merge operator
(
E_{1}\sqcup E_{2}
) described below (used to handle branching) is partial,
and because EPP ensures that variables are only used by locations that have bound them.
We denote the cases where a choreography fails to project with the notation “
undefined
,” leaving failures due to the merge operator implicit.

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\rho.e\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\rho.e\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\rho.e\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\rho.e\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e})&\text{if}~L\in\rho\\
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}~\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}&\text{if}~L\in\rho\\
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}\mathrel{\fatsemi}\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}\mathrel{\fatsemi}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fun}}}_{\rho}{F}({X\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mu\tau})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fun}}}_{\rho}{F}({X\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mu\tau})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fun}}}_{\rho}{F}({X\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mu\tau})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fun}}}_{\rho}{F}({X\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mu\tau})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}}&\text{if}~L\in\rho\\
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}&\text{if}~L\notin\rho~\text{and}~\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}\neq\text{undefined}\\
\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}~{\{\alpha\}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[\alpha\mapsto L\right]}}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}{C[\alpha\mapsto L]}\rrbracket_{L}}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}}}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern \displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}&\text{if}~L=\ell\\
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}\mathrel{\fatsemi}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\ell}&\text{if}~L\neq\ell~\text{and}~L\in\rho\\
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}C\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choose}}~{d}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}for}}~{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}}&\text{if}~L=\ell\\
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}~({d}\Rightarrow{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}})&\text{if}~L\neq\ell~\text{and}~L\in\rho\\
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\

{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}}\end{array}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}_{\rho}~{C}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}\\
