{}\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}}\end{array}\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\mathrlap{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}localCase}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}})~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}})\hskip 9.24994pt\text{if}~L\in\rho}&\\
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}\mathrel{\fatsemi}(\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}\sqcup\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}})&\text{if}~L\notin\rho~\text{and}~x\notin\operatorname{fv}({\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}})~\text{and}~y\notin\operatorname{fv}({\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{2}\rrbracket_{L}}})\\
\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\ell}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\ell}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\ell}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\ell}.\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}()~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C}\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\alpha}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\alpha}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\alpha}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\alpha}}})~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}}&\text{if}~L=\ell\\

\text{undefined}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L^{\prime}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L^{\prime}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L^{\prime}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L^{\prime}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C}\rrbracket_{L}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}\text{undefined}&\text{if}~L=L^{\prime}\\
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Figure 10.
Selected EPP Definitions

EPP is defined in a structurally-recursive manner over the syntax of choreographies.
The rules are very similar to those of
\lambda
qc

(
Samuelson et al. 2025
)
, with the exception of functions
and applications, where
\lambda

\pitchfork

accounts for the annotations allowing a subset of
locations to participate in a function body.
The majority of rules simply convert choreographic syntax into the network language equivalent,
but a few cases—such as those in Figure
10
—are more complex.

For the local computation
\rho.e
, only locations in
\rho
should compute
e
while others do nothing.
For functions whose bodies may involve locations from
\rho
, only those locations in
\rho
project to a function,
while others can simply project to a unit value
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}
.
The projection of function applications is similar, where locations involved in the function body
should apply the function, while other locations can simply sequence the function and its argument,
afterwards returning a unit value.
The projection of functions requires an additional check compared to previous work—that the body projects for everyone—for reasons described below.

Type functions project to network type functions,
but those that abstract over locations (and location sets) must behave differently depending on whether or not the variable
\alpha
resolves to
L
.
Following
Graversen et al. 2024
and
Samuelson et al. 2025
, we use
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}
to branch on the identity of the current process.
In the
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}
branch, when the locations match, we substitute
\alpha
with
\ell
in the body
C
before projecting
C
.
In the
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}
branch, we project the body directly.
Because location variables are equal only to themselves, this projection correctly treats
\alpha\neq L
.

For the send
C\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho
, all locations first execute their projection of
C
,
then location
\ell
multicasts the output of
C
to the locations in
\rho
, who receive it.
For the selection statement
{\ell}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}[}{d}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}]}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}C
, location
\ell
sends the choice
d
to all in
\rho
,
who condition on this choice using an
\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow-choice}}}
.
Because the choice is certain,
\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow-choice}}}
only has that branch.

For
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}
expressions, first all locations execute the program in the guard, then locations in
\rho
branch on its value.
For non-branching locations, the merge operator
(E_{1}\sqcup E_{2})

(
Montesi 2023
)
combines the two branches.
Merge is an idempotent binary partial function defined homomorphically on matching network programs.
It collects
\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow-choice}}}
branches that exist on only one side, and merges those that exist in both.
For instance,

\left(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}~({\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}})\right)\sqcup\left(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}~({\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}})\right)\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}~(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}})~(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}}).

Our merge operator is identical to that of
\lambda
qc
,
accounting for the additions of
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}
and
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}
.
Its full definition can be found in Appendix
D.1
.
Since non-branching locations don’t know the scrutinee,
EPP also checks that local variables
x
and
y
are not free in the projection of the branches.
The choreographic
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}case}}
expressions has identical rules.

For the new
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expression, the parent location
\ell
projects to a network
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}
expression with two pieces of code.
The body is the projection of the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
’s body to
\ell
,
and the thread task is the projection of the body to the child thread
\alpha
.
That is, the parent projects the body twice: once for its own role,
and a second time for the role of the spawned thread.
Locations not equal to the parent can simply project to the body of the
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fork}}
expression, ensuring that
neither of the two variables bound in the body are free.

To project a
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
expression, everyone must perform their role to execute the body.
However, we leave the projection for the spawned thread
L^{\prime}
as explicitly
undefined
.
As explained below in Section
6.2
, naïvely
projecting the entire choreography for a spawned thread can incorrectly produce extra code
that they do not have access to.
We thus handle projecting spawned threads with a restricted EPP function defined below that excises this superfluous code
and ensures
L^{\prime}
does not attempt to directly project
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L^{\prime}}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C}
.

Instead of directly using the sequencing primitive
E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{2}
,
note that EPP must use the
collapsing sequencing function

E_{1}\mathrel{\fatsemi}E_{2}
introduced by
Samuelson et al. 2025
, which is defined as

E_{1}\mathrel{\fatsemi}E_{2}=E_{2}
when
E_{1}
is a value, and is otherwise
E_{1}\mathrel{\fatsemi}E_{2}=E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{2}
.
It may seem that this function is only an optimization,
but it is actually required to ensure projected programs can correctly simulate out-of-order choreographic steps.

Combining this collapsing sequencing operator with the involved location tracking necessary to maintain deadlock freedom
also allows us to project entire choreographies to
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}
for uninvolved parties.
We therefore, essentially for free, achieve the main goal of
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2023
’s [
(
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2023
)
] modular endpoint projection—that
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}
should not depend on parts of
C
that do not involve
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
.

Projecting Functions

There are two important notes to be made about the projection of functions and type functions.
First, because any location might participate in the body of a process-polymorphic type function,
we require all locations to project these functions.

Second, for standard functions, if
L\notin\rho
, the function projects to
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}
because
L
will not participate in the body,
but we still require
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}
to be
defined
.
This requirement may seem unnecessary; if
L
does not participate in the body,
one might hope that the body would always project, preferably to
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}
.
Unfortunately this is not so, which can cause otherwise-projectable choreographies to step to non-projectable ones.
To see why, consider the type function
C={\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\Lambda}\ell\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}}\ldotp\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}if}}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}~{X}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}then}}~{\ell.4}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}else}}~{\ell.(3\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}*}}2)}

which has type
\forall{\ell\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{loc}}}[{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\ell\}}]\ldotp{\mathsf{int}@\ell}
in context
X\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mu\mathsf{bool}@\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
.
While
C
projects for
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
, it does not project for any other location—for instance,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
.
The
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}
branch of the resulting
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}
would need to merge
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({4})\sqcup\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({3\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}*}}2})
, which is undefined.
Wrapping
C
in a function and applying it gives the well-typed choreography

C^{\prime}=({\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\lambda}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}~X\ldotp(C\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}))\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}
involving only
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
.
If we did not check that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
could project the function,

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
’s projection of
C^{\prime}
would be
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}\mathrel{\fatsemi}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}\mathrel{\fatsemi}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}
since he is completely uninvolved.
However, after
\beta
-reducing
C^{\prime}
, the program no longer projects for
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
,
since
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}\right]}}{C[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}]}{C[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}]}{C[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}]}\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}\right]}}{C[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}]}{C[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}]}{C[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}]}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}\right]}}{C[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}]}{C[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}]}{C[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}]}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket\mathchoice{C{\left[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}\right]}}{C[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}]}{C[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}]}{C[X\mapsto\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}true}]}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
is undefined.
In reality, by (unsuccessfully) checking that
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}}}
is defined initially, we statically reject the program.

6.2.
Projecting Systems and Active Threads

While the above EPP definition produces a network program for a single location,
choreographies specify the behavior of many participants.
To produce a system of network programs (see Section
5.2.1
), we lift EPP to a finite set of locations
\Omega\subset\mathcal{L}
.
Non-ephemeral locations compile the entire choreography before executing it,
so we can simply take the projection as defined above.
For spawned threads, however, we must restrict to the code available to that location—the subexpression provided by its parent—not the whole choreography.
Because we track the scope of threads with
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
,
we use their presence to identify when a process has a scoped lifetime and which code such processes need.
More formally, we define a modified version of EPP, denoted
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}
, to handle spawned threads as follows.

\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}\triangleq\begin{cases}(\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{1}\rrbracket_{L}}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C_{n}\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{n}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{n}\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C_{n}\rrbracket_{L}})\mathrel{\fatsemi}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}&\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\text{if}~C~\text{contains}~n>0~\text{subterms of the form}\\
\quad\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C_{1}},\ldots,\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{C_{n}}\end{array}\\
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket_{L}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

The only complexity is that, due to out-of-order steps,
a thread may be simultaneously spawned in both branches of a case-expression,
so the
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
corresponding to a thread is only unique
up-to branching
.
The modified EPP function handles this wrinkle similarly to projecting
non-branching locations in a
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}case}}
or
\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}localCase}}
:
it extracts all
\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill-after}}}
bodies for a thread
and merges together their projections followed by an
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}
.
The full definition of
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}
can be found in Appendix
D.3
.

To project a full system, we then lift this restricted EPP point-wise to each running location.
That is, for a finite
\Omega\subset\mathcal{L}
, we define
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}\triangleq{\parallel_{L\in\Omega}(L\triangleright\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}})}
.
Note that
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{L}}
must be defined for all
L\in\Omega
for
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}
to be defined.

6.3.
Completeness, Weak Soundness, and Deadlock Freedom

We have now provided two separate semantics for
\lambda

\pitchfork

: the top-level choreographic semantics (Section
4.2
), and the semantics given by EPP.
Here we examine the relationship between these semantics and use that relationship to provide a deadlock-freedom guarantee for compiled systems.

Simulation Relation

To relate the two semantics, we must decide which systems are related to a given choreography.
While one may think a choreography
C
should only relate to its projection
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}
, this property is not preserved by reductions.
Specifically during branching steps, the branch not taken is discarded in the choreography,
but is retained in projected programs waiting on a selection message.
Additionally, EPP’s use of the collapsing sequencing function
E_{1}\mathrel{\fatsemi}E_{2}

means programs that resolve to a value after a substitution may be removed from the projected program.

To account for these mismatches we follow traditional choreographic style
(
Montesi 2023
)
by defining a relation
E_{1}\preceq E_{2}

which relates two network programs if
E_{1}
may have discarded unneeded code—choices or sequenced values—that remain in
E_{2}
.
Formally, it is the smallest structurally compatible partial order on network programs that admits the following three rules.

\frac{E_{1}\preceq E_{1}^{\prime}}{\begin{array}[]{@{\mkern 2mu}l@{\mkern 2mu}}.0mu\lx@intercol\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\\
.0mu\lx@intercol\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\end{array}\preceq\begin{array}[]{@{\mkern 2mu}l@{\mkern 2mu}}.0mu\lx@intercol\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\\
.0mu\lx@intercol\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}^{\prime}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\\
.0mu\lx@intercol\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}^{\prime}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\end{array}}\frac{E_{2}\preceq E_{2}^{\prime}}{\begin{array}[]{@{\mkern 2mu}l@{\mkern 2mu}}.0mu\lx@intercol\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\\
.0mu\lx@intercol\mid{\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\end{array}\preceq\begin{array}[]{@{\mkern 2mu}l@{\mkern 2mu}}.0mu\lx@intercol\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\\
.0mu\lx@intercol\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1}^{\prime}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\\
.0mu\lx@intercol\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2}^{\prime}}\hfil\mkern 2.0mu\end{array}}\frac{E_{1}\preceq E_{2}\quad\operatorname{Val}({V})}{E_{1}\preceq V\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{2}}

To extend this relation to entire systems we lift it point-wise:
\Pi_{1}\preceq\Pi_{2}~\triangleq~\forall L\in\Omega\ldotp\Pi_{1}(L)\preceq\Pi_{2}(L).

This relaxed correspondence is sufficient to prove that the projected semantics simulate the choreographic semantics.

Theorem 5 (Completeness).

If
\Theta\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho
and
\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\subseteq\Omega
,
then whenever
\mathchoice{\left\langle{C},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}
,
there is some
\Pi^{\prime}
such that
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{S}}{}\Pi^{\prime}
and
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}\preceq\Pi^{\prime}
.

In addition to this completeness theorem, one might expect a corresponding soundness theorem.
However, the combination of divergent programs and multiply-located computations makes a standard soundness theorem impossible.
Consider the choreography
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}loop}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}2)
.
The only possible choreographic step is to run the infinite loop at
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
.
In the projected system, however,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
can immediately compute
3
.

Samuelson et al. 2025
avoid this difficulty by both requiring all locations to synchronize at every function boundary
and only showing soundness when no local computation in the choreography diverges.
A similar approach fails here, as
\lambda

\pitchfork

allows a selected subset of locations to participate in a
\beta
-reduction,
meaning that even a
choreographic
function can loop for
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
while allowing
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
to proceed.
This dilemma has existed since the advent of functional choreographic programming
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
;
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2023
)
,
and while recent work has proposed a choreographic semantics which avoids these non-termination–related issues
(
Plyukhin et al. 2025
)
,
further research is needed to adapt this semantics to the features of
\lambda

\pitchfork

.
See Section
7
for more discussion.

Rather than enforcing global synchronization to satisfy a rigid proof technique—which would negate many benefits of parallelism
and possibly require locations who do not even know each other exist to synchronize—we instead establish a robust soundness property that tolerates the divergence described above.
This theorem acknowledges that the projected system may execute some operations that the choreography will not.
However, it guarantees that the choreography can match any system step,
provided that step is not blocked at the choreographic level by a computation that has not yet finished in the system.
In the above example,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
computing
3
is just such a blocked step, as
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}loop}
has not—and will never—complete at the system level.

Making matters worse, some choreographic steps correspond to multiple system steps,
and the system may have taken only some of them.
To match these system steps, the choreography might execute in ways that step some locations
more
than the system did.
In essence, the best the choreography can do may be “ahead” at some locations and “behind” at others.
For instance, in the example above, if
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
steps
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}loop}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
computes
3
,
but
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
does nothing, the choreography cannot match
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}
’s step,
and while it can match
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}
’s steps, doing so will place it ahead at
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
.

To formalize this notion, we say if
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{S}}\Pi
,
then
C
must step to some
C^{\prime}
whose projection is behind
\Pi

only at locations that cannot step
C^{\prime}
,
but might be ahead of
\Pi
elsewhere.

Theorem 6 (Divergence-Weakened Soundness).

If
\,\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho
,

\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\subseteq\Omega
,
and
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{S}}\Pi
,
then there is some
C^{\prime}
,
\Omega^{\prime}
,
\Pi^{\prime}
,
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
, and trace
t
of system labels such that

(1)

\mathchoice{\left\langle{C},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}
and
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{S}}\Pi^{\prime}
with
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}\preceq\Pi^{\prime}
,

(2)

\Pi\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{S}}\Pi^{\prime\prime}
and
\Pi^{\prime}\mathrel{{\overset{t\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}^{*}_{S}}\Pi^{\prime\prime}
, and

(3)

for all
C^{\prime\prime}
,
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
, and
R
, if
\mathchoice{\left\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\overset{\smash{R}\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{C^{\prime\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime\prime}}\rangle}
, then
\operatorname{rloc}({R})\cap\operatorname{loc}({t})=\varnothing
.

The following diagram visualizes the relationships provided by conditions (
1
) and (
2
).
\mathchoice{\left\langle{C},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}
\mathchoice{\left\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}\preceq\Pi^{\prime}
\Pi
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}}
\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}}{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}}
\scriptstyle*
\scriptstyle*
\scriptstyle*
\scriptstyle*
\scriptstyle t
\scriptstyle*

Allowing
\Pi
and
\Pi^{\prime}
to take additional steps to reach
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
allows
C^{\prime}
to be “ahead” of
\Pi
at some locations and “behind” at others, respectively.
However, condition (
3
) captures the idea that
\Pi^{\prime}
—and thus
C^{\prime}
—can only be behind at locations where
C^{\prime}
is blocked and cannot (yet) step.

While divergence-weakened soundness is weaker than prior soundness results
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
;
Samuelson et al. 2025
)
,
it is achievable with multiply-located computations and no implicit synchronization,
and is strong enough to prove deadlock freedom.
Specifically, by combining type soundness (Theorems
2
,
3
, and
4
),
EPP completeness (Theorem
5
),
Theorem
6
,
and a standard parallel confluence lemma (Lemma
90
) common to concurrent systems,
we can prove the following theorem.

Theorem 7 (Deadlock Freedom).

If
\,\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}C\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}:}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}{\mathrel{\rhd}}}\rho
and
\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\subseteq\Omega
,
then whenever
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}{\llbracket C\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega}}\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{S}}\Pi
,
either
\Pi
is final—every location in
\Pi
is a value—or
\Pi
can step.

Proof Sketch.

Begin by applying Theorem
6
to get
C^{\prime}
,
\Omega^{\prime}
,
\Pi^{\prime}
,
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
, and
t
, as specified in the theorem.
Since
\Pi\mathrel{\Longrightarrow^{*}_{S}}\Pi^{\prime\prime}
, if it requires one or more steps, then
\Pi
steps and we are done.
Otherwise, we get
\Pi=\Pi^{\prime\prime}
.
Type soundness ensures either
C^{\prime}
is a value or
\mathchoice{\left\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}
can step.
If
C^{\prime}
is a value, then
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}
is final.
Since
\mathchoice{\left\llbracket C^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}{\llbracket C^{\prime}\rrbracket^{\pitchfork}_{\Omega^{\prime}}}\preceq\Pi^{\prime}
, then
\Pi^{\prime}
can step to a final system, so
\Pi=\Pi^{\prime\prime}
can too.

Finally, if
\mathchoice{\left\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}\mathrel{{\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}[2.0pt]{$\scriptstyle R$}\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{C^{\prime\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime\prime}}\rangle}
,
EPP completeness ensures that
\Pi^{\prime}
can step with system label(s) corresponding to
R
.
However, condition (
3
) of Theorem
6
guarantees that
\operatorname{rloc}({R})\cap\operatorname{loc}({t})=\varnothing
.
That is, the step(s) from
R
involve a disjoint set of locations from those taking
\Pi^{\prime}
to
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
,
so the parallel confluence lemma says that we can take them in either order to get the same result.
Thus,
\Pi=\Pi^{\prime\prime}
can take the
R
step(s) and is not deadlocked.
∎

7.
Related Work

While
\lambda

\pitchfork

is the first functional choreographic language with process forking, it builds on a rich literature which we review here.
First, we discuss the development of functional choreographic programming, including process polymorphism.
We then compare the approach for process spawning in
\lambda

\pitchfork

to previous (lower-order) choreographic languages.
Finally, we look at process spawning in multiparty session types, the main alternative to choreographic programming.

7.1.
Functional Choreographic Programming

Since its inception
(
Carbone and Montesi 2013
;
Montesi 2013
)
, the choreographic-programming paradigm has advanced considerably.
Early work expanded on core features such as local computations, message passing, and recursion
(
Carbone et al. 2014
;
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2018
;
Cruz-Filipe and Montesi 2017a
;
Lanese et al. 2013
;
Cruz-Filipe and Montesi 2017b
, see e.g.,)
,
but only allowed imperative and procedural computation.

Pirouette
(
Hirsch and Garg 2022
)
and Chor
\lambda

(
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2022
)
—developed independently—were the first functional choreographic languages.

Bates et al. 2025
extended Chor
\lambda
with multiply-located values, providing an alternative to synchronization messages.

Graversen et al. 2024
introduced process polymorphism.

Samuelson et al. 2025
developed
\lambda
qc
—the language
\lambda

\pitchfork

primarily extends—to provide process
-set
polymorphism and first-class location names for the first time.
This last feature was critical for the development of
\lambda

\pitchfork

.

To prove projection sound, Pirouette and
\lambda
qc
relied on global synchronization at every function boundary.

Cruz-Filipe et al. 2023
provided an out-of-order semantics for Chor
\lambda
where EPP is sound and complete,
but relied on fragile rewrite rules that fail in the presence of named recursive functions
(
Samuelson et al. 2025
)
.

Plyukhin et al. 2025
introduced
semilenient
evaluation for a subset of Chor
\lambda
including recursive (choreographic) functions.
Semilenient evaluation does not rely on the fragile rewrite rules used by previous work,
yielding a clean resolution to the tension between allowing non-termination, avoiding global synchronization, and providing a sound choreographic semantics.
It is, however, not obvious how to generalize the principles of the semilenient strategy to support multiply-located computations.
For example, applying a simple generalization of the semilenient strategy to the choreography below leaves it stuck on the
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}loop}
,
failing to capture the fact that
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}
will fully evaluate their projected program to 3 even though the send never occurs.

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}loop}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641};}}\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{(\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B}\}.2\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C})}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}A},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}B},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}C}\}.(1\mathbin{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}+}}x)}

We instead introduce
divergence-weakened soundness
,
a weaker result that holds for
\lambda

\pitchfork

’s multiply-located computations without synchronization,
but remains sufficient to prove deadlock freedom.

7.2.
Process Spawning in Choreographies

Two papers of which we are aware have previously considered process spawning in lower-order choreographies.
The first, by
Carbone and Montesi 2013
, allows service threads to be declared at the top-level.
These threads then wait for a notification from a parent process to begin execution.
Thus the number of threads is statically chosen, and new ones cannot be dynamically spawned.

Cruz-Filipe and Montesi 2017b
provided a highly tailored calculus to implement parallel divide-and-conquer algorithms.
It was able to allow for truly dynamic process spawning,
and as a result provide processes like parallel merge sort, which the earlier work of
Carbone and Montesi 2013
could not.
Moreover, it relies on a heavily restricted language: the only form of abstraction provided are (recursive) top-level process polymorphic functions,
and there is no notion of variable binding.
Parent processes can communicate the names of spawned threads to others, but process names are otherwise an entirely second-class notion.

Cruz-Filipe and Montesi 2017b
does use a
behavioral
type system to ensure that processes have been properly introduced prior to communicating.
However, because of the restrictive nature of their calculus, they do not have to worry about many of the problems addressed in this paper, including scoped processes, closures that capture dead processes, and the combination of spawning with general process-polymorphic functions.
Moreover, while parent processes can communicate the names of spawned threads, the lack of first-class process names means that their potential communication patterns are significantly restricted.
In contrast, this work uses a
data
type system to address the issues, like those mentioned previously, that emerge when combining process spawning with higher-order choreographies.

7.3.
Process Spawning in (Multiparty) Session Types

Concurrent programming has always had process spawning as a major feature
(
Milner et al. 1992
;
Milner 1980
)
, and thus it has always been prevalent in session types
(
Honda 1993
;
Honda et al. 1998
;
Gay and Vasconcelos 2010
;
Wadler 2012
;
Caires and Pfenning 2010
)
.
Traditionally, session types either do not guarantee deadlock freedom
(
Honda 1993
;
Honda et al. 1998
)
or require that processes only communicate in an acyclic topology
(
Caires and Pfenning 2010
;
Wadler 2012
)
.

Multiparty
session types address this deficit, but only for a particular set of communicating processes.
To allow for process spawning, they must create a new session which includes the new process, and then reason about communication orders between sessions.
This leads to complicated reasoning principles
(
Jacobs et al. 2022
;
Coppo et al. 2016
;
Coppo et al. 2013
;
Bettini et al. 2008
)
.
Recently,
Le Brun et al. 2025
considered multiparty session types with replication, which allows new processes to spawn in the same session.
However, the processes that can be spawned via replication are limited.
In particular, they do not allow paradigms such as parallel divide-and-conquer.

8.
Conclusion

This work introduced
\lambda

\pitchfork

, the first functional choreographic language to support process forking.

\lambda

\pitchfork

retains support for key features of
\lambda
qc
—its predecessor—including higher-order programming, process polymorphism, multiply-located computations, and first-class process names.
While this combination of features is powerful, it can introduce complex bugs,
such as killing a thread and then attempting to execute a function that closes over its name.

\lambda

\pitchfork

prevents these bugs by integrating participant tracking into its type system.

\lambda

\pitchfork

can model complex multiparty computations where arbitrarily many threads are spawned and killed, including a fork bomb.
Despite this, we retain the classic choreographic result that the projection of every well-typed choreography is deadlock-free,
yielding the first deadlock freedom proof for a choreography with multiply-located computations and no implicit synchronization.

A.6.
Redex for an Evaluation Context

If
\eta
is an evaluation context and
R
is a redex, we define
\eta[R]
to be the redex which corresponds to making the reduction given by
R
in the context
\eta
.

{[\boldsymbol{\cdot}]}[R]\triangleq R{(\eta\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}C)}[R]\triangleq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}Fun}}({{\eta}[R]}){(V\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}\eta)}[R]\triangleq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}Arg}}({{\eta}[R]}){(\eta~t)_{\rho}}[R]\triangleq R{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fold}}_{\rho}~{\eta})}[R]={(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}unfold}}_{\rho}~{\eta})}[R]\triangleq R{(\eta,C)_{\rho}}[R]\triangleq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}PairL}}({{\eta}[R]}){(V,\eta)_{\rho}}[R]\triangleq\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}PairR}}({{\eta}[R]}){(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fst}}_{\rho}~{\eta})}[R]={(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}snd}}_{\rho}~{\eta})}[R]\triangleq{\eta}[R]{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inl}}_{\rho}~{\eta})}[R]={(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inr}}_{\rho}~{\eta})}[R]\triangleq{\eta}[R]{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}case}}_{\rho}~{\eta}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}of}}~(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inl}}~{X}\Rightarrow{C_{1}})~(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}inr}}~{Y}\Rightarrow{C_{2}}))}[R]\triangleq{\eta}[R]{(\eta\mathrel{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}{\mkern 3.0mu\raisebox{0.21529pt}{$\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\{}\ell{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\}}$}\mkern-1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\rightsquigarrow}}}\rho)}[R]\triangleq{\eta}[R]{(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\rho.x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\eta}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C})}[R]={(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}let}}~{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{\eta}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}in}}~{C})}[R]\triangleq{\eta}[R]{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}kill}}~{L}~\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}after}}~{\eta}}[R]\triangleq{\eta}[R]

A.7.
Location Set Operations

Here we define the containment
\ell\in\rho
, disjointness
\rho_{1}\cap\rho_{2}=\varnothing
, and subset
\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho_{2}
relations;
and the set-difference function
\rho_{1}\setminus\rho_{2}
,
with special care given to how they are defined when the locations and sets in question are open terms.

The principle for how the containment and subset relations behave is that of a modality of
necessity
.
For instance, the containment relation
\ell\in\rho
holds only if the location
\ell
is an element of
\rho
for
all
values that their variables could resolve to.
Note here that the metavariable
\ell
stands for either a type variable
\alpha
or a concrete location
L\in\mathcal{L}
, and the metavariable
\rho
stands for any location set, including possibly a type variable.

\frac{~}{\ell\in\{\ell\}}\frac{\ell\in\rho_{1}}{\ell\in\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}}\frac{\ell\in\rho_{2}}{\ell\in\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}}\frac{~}{\ell\in\top}

The disjointness relation is defined as expected:

(\rho_{1}\cap\rho_{2}=\varnothing)\triangleq\forall\ell\ldotp\neg(\ell\in\rho_{1}\wedge\ell\in\rho_{2})

To define the subset relation, we first note that we cannot use the naïve definition in terms of the containment relation.
That is,
\forall\ell\ldotp\ell\in\rho_{1}\Rightarrow\ell\in\rho_{2}
would not serve as a correct definition for
\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho_{2}
in the presence of type variables.
Specifically, because
\ell\notin\alpha
for every location
\ell
, with this definition we would have that
\alpha\subseteq\rho
for every set
\rho
.
The relation should have the modality of necessity (i.e., be preserved under substitution),
but this example shows that this is not the case with the naïve definition.
Instead, the subset relation must be defined inductively as follows.

\frac{~}{\varnothing\subseteq\rho}\frac{~}{\alpha\subseteq\alpha}\frac{\ell\in\rho}{\{\ell\}\subseteq\rho}\frac{\rho\subseteq\rho_{1}}{\rho\subseteq\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}}\frac{\rho\subseteq\rho_{2}}{\rho\subseteq\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}}\frac{\rho_{1}\subseteq\rho\quad\rho_{2}\subseteq\rho}{\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}\subseteq\rho}\frac{~}{\rho\subseteq\top}

Finally, the set subtraction function
\rho_{1}\setminus\rho_{2}
is defined inductively on
\rho_{1}
—taking advantage of the relations defined above—to remove all components from
\rho_{1}
that appear in
\rho_{2}
.

\varnothing\setminus\rho=\varnothing\alpha\setminus\rho=\begin{cases}\varnothing&\alpha\subseteq\rho\\
\alpha&\alpha\not\subseteq\rho\end{cases}\{\ell\}\setminus\rho=\begin{cases}\varnothing&\ell\in\rho\\
\{\ell\}&\ell\notin\rho\end{cases}(\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2})\setminus\rho=(\rho_{1}\setminus\rho)\cup(\rho_{2}\setminus\rho)\top\setminus\rho=\begin{cases}\varnothing&\top\subseteq\rho\\
\top&\top\not\subseteq\rho\end{cases}

A.8.
Choreography Operational Semantics

\frac{\mathchoice{\left\langle{C},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{C},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\overset{R\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{C^{\prime}},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}}{\mathchoice{\left\langle{\eta[C]},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{\eta[C]},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\eta[C]},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\eta[C]},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{\eta}[R]}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{\eta[C^{\prime}]},{\Omega^{\prime}}\right\rangle}{\langle{\eta[C^{\prime}]},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{\eta[C^{\prime}]},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}{\langle{\eta[C^{\prime}]},{\Omega^{\prime}}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[C-Ctx]},vcenter}\frac{e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e_{2}\quad\quad\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\subseteq\Omega\quad\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing}{\mathchoice{\left\langle{\rho.e_{1}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{\rho.e_{1}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\rho.e_{1}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\rho.e_{1}},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{{\rho}.({e_{1}}\rightarrow{e_{2}})}}{}_{c}}\mathchoice{\left\langle{\rho.e_{2}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{\rho.e_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\rho.e_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\rho.e_{2}},{\Omega}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{lab={\footnotesize[C-Done]},vcenter}\frac{f=\operatorname{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}fun}}}_{\rho}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\coloneqq}}{C}\quad\operatorname{Val}({V})\quad\quad\operatorname{NL}({\rho})\subseteq\Omega\quad\operatorname{fv}({\rho})=\varnothing}{\mathchoice{\left\langle{f\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}V},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{f\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}V},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{f\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}V},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{f\mathbin{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\$}}_{\rho}}V},{\Omega}\rangle}\mathrel{{\xRightarrow{\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}App}}_{\ V]}},{\Omega}\right\rangle}{\langle{\mathchoice{C{\left[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V\right]}}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\mathchoice{C{\left[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V\right]}}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}},{\Omega}\rangle}{\langle{\mathchoice{C{\left[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V\right]}}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto V]}{C[F\mapsto f,\mkern 2.0muX\mapsto Appendix B
Static Semantics

B.1.
\lambda

\pitchfork

Kinding System

First we note that, in order to prevent kind dependency, the kinding
context should be split into two contexts—
\Gamma_{\ell}
for locations and location sets of kind
\kappa_{\ell}\in\{*_{\mathsf{loc}},*_{\mathsf{locset}},*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}\}
,
and
\Gamma
for local and program kinds.
We have elided this detail from the presentation of the kinding and typing rules for simplicity,
but fully address the details in Appendix
E.1
and subsequent appendices.
Our kinding system is designed so that the subkinding relationship
*_{\mathsf{locset}}<:*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}

and relevant subkinding rule—while not explicitly given—is admissible (see Lemma
25
).

\frac{\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\Gamma\quad\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa\in\Gamma}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\alpha\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}\kappa}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-Var]},vcenter}\frac{\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\Gamma\quad\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}\in\Gamma}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\alpha\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-SubVar]},vcenter}\frac{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}\in\mathcal{L}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.7109,0.0977,0.3867}L}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{loc}}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-Loc]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\ell\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{loc}}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\{\ell\}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-Sng]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\ell\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{loc}}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\{\ell\}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-SngFin]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-Union]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-UnionFin]},vcenter}\frac{\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\Gamma}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\top\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-AnySet]},vcenter}\frac{\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\Gamma\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}\Vdash}t_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}::}*_{e}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}t_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{e}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-Local]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}t_{e}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{e}\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}t_{e}@\rho\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-At]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}\quad\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{1}}\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{2}}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}\xrightarrow{\rho}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}\cup\rho}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-Arrow]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{1}}\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{2}}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-Prod]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{1}}\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{2}}\quad\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}\quad\rho_{1}\cup\rho_{2}\subseteq\rho}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau_{1}+_{\rho}\tau_{2}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-Sum]},vcenter}\frac{\Gamma,\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\rho}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\mu_{\rho}\alpha\ldotp\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-Rec]},vcenter}\frac{\kappa\in\{*_{\mathsf{loc}},*_{\mathsf{locset}},*_{\mathsf{finlocset}}\}\quad\quad\Gamma,\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{\tau}}\quad\Gamma,\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}\quad}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\forall{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}[{\rho}]\ldotp{\tau}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\top}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-AllLoc]},vcenter}\frac{\kappa\in\{*_{e},*_{\rho^{\prime}}\}\quad\quad\Gamma,\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\tau\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{\tau}}\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\rho\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\mathsf{locset}}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}\vdash}\forall{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}[{\rho}]\ldotp{\tau}\mathrel{\color[rgb]{0,0.3242,0.6641}::}*_{\rho_{\tau}\cup\rho}}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[K-All]},vcenter}

B.2.
Context Projection

For a context
\Theta=\Gamma_{\ell};\Gamma;\Delta_{e};\Delta
, define its projection
\left.\Theta\right|_{\rho}

to a (possibly open) location set
\rho
to be the local context
\left.\Theta\right|_{\rho}=\Gamma_{\ell};\left.\Gamma\right|_{\rho};\left.\Delta_{e}\right|_{\rho}
,
where
\left.\Gamma\right|_{\rho}
and
\left.\Delta_{e}\right|_{\rho}
are defined as follows.

\left.\cdot\right|_{\rho}\triangleq\cdot\left.(\Gamma,\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa_{\rho^{\prime}})\right|_{\rho}\triangleq\left.\Gamma\right|_{\rho}\left.(\Gamma,\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa_{e})\right|_{\rho}\triangleq\left.\Gamma\right|_{\rho},\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa_{e}\left.(\Delta_{e},\rho^{\prime}.x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e})\right|_{\rho}\triangleq\begin{cases}\left.\Delta_{e}\right|_{\rho},x\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern 2.0mut_{e}&\rho\subseteq\rho^{\prime}\\
\left.\Delta_{e}\right|_{\rho}&\rho\not\subseteq\rho^{\prime}\end{cases}

B.3.
\lambda

\pitchfork

Type System

In these rules we denote
\Theta=\Gamma;\Delta_{e};\Delta
for brevity,
and abuse notation to add variables to and use the kinding judgment on the relevant sub-contexts of
\Theta

as appropriate.
In the subsequent sections we may use the shorthand
\operatorname{tloc}({\Theta};{\tau})
to denote the set
\rho
of locations such that
\Theta\vdash\tau\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu*_{\rho}
.

\frac{\vdash\Theta\quad X\mkern 2.0mu{:}\mkern Appendix C
Network Language

C.1.
Network Language Expressions

\begin{array}[]{@{} >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}c >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l @{}}\textrm{Network Program}&E&\Coloneqq&X\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e})\,\mid\,E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{2}\\
&&\,\mid&\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E}\,\mid\,E_{1}~E_{2}\,\mid\,\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E}\,\mid\,E~t\\
&&\,\mid&(E_{1},E_{2})\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fst}}~{E}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}snd}}~{E}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{E}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{E}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}case}}~{E}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}~(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{X}\Rightarrow{E_{1}})~(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{Y}\Rightarrow{E_{2}})\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}localCase}}~{E}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{E_{1}})~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{E_{2}})\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fold}}~{E}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}unfold}}~{E}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{E}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\ell}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2}}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2}}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choose}}~{d}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}for}}~{\ell}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}{E}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}~(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}\Rightarrow{{E_{1}}_{\bot}})~(\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\Rightarrow{{E_{2}}_{\bot}})\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}~{\rho}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2}}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({E_{1}})~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2}}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}\\
\textrm{Network Values}&V&\Coloneqq&X\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({v})\,\mid\,\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E}\,\mid\,\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E}\\
&&\,\mid&(V_{1},V_{2})\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{V}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{V}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fold}}~{V}\end{array}

C.2.
Transition Labels and Evaluation Contexts

\begin{array}[]{@{} >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}c >{\@pl@syntax@ignorePar}l @{}}\textrm{Transition Labels}&l&\Coloneqq&\iota\,\mid\,{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}{\rho}\,\mid\,{L}.{m}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\rightsquigarrow}}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({L},{E})\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}\\
\\
\textrm{Evaluation Contexts}&\eta&\Coloneqq&[\boldsymbol{\cdot}]\,\mid\,\eta\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E\,\mid\,\eta~E\,\mid\,V~\eta\,\mid\,\eta~t\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fold}}~{\eta}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}unfold}}~{\eta}\\
&&\,\mid&(\eta,E)\,\mid\,(V,\eta)\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fst}}~{\eta}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}snd}}~{\eta}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{\eta}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{\eta}\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}case}}~{\eta}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}~(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{X}\Rightarrow{E_{1}})~(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{Y}\Rightarrow{E_{2}})\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}localCase}}~{\eta}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{E_{1}})~(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{E_{2}})\\
&&\,\mid&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{\eta}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{\eta}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E}\,\mid\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{\eta}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E}\end{array}

C.3.
Network Language Operational Semantics

\frac{L\triangleright E_{1}\mathrel{{\overset{l\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}}E_{2}}{L\triangleright\eta[E_{1}]\mathrel{{\overset{l\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}}\eta[E_{2}]}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-Ctx]},vcenter}\frac{e_{1}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}e_{2}}{L\triangleright\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e_{1}})\mathrel{{\overset{\iota\mkern 4.0mu}{\Longrightarrow}}{}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e_{2}})}\quad\textsc{left={\footnotesize[N-Ret]},vcenter}\frac{\operatorname{Val}({V})}{L\triangleright V\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E\mathrel{{\overset{\iota\mkern Appendix D
Compilation

D.1.
Network Program Merging

We show the patterns for which
E_{1}\sqcup E_{2}
is defined; if there is no matching pattern, then
E_{1}\sqcup E_{2}
is undefined.

\displaystyle\text{undefined}\sqcup\text{undefined}

\displaystyle\triangleq\text{undefined}

\displaystyle\text{undefined}\sqcup E_{2}

\displaystyle\triangleq E_{2}

\displaystyle E_{1}\sqcup\text{undefined}

\displaystyle\triangleq E_{1}

\displaystyle X\sqcup X

\displaystyle\triangleq X

\displaystyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}\sqcup\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}()}}

\displaystyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e})\sqcup\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}ret}}({e})

\displaystyle(E_{1,1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{1,2})\sqcup(E_{2,1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{2,2})

\displaystyle\triangleq E_{1,1}\sqcup E_{2,1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}E_{1,2}\sqcup E_{2,2}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{1}})\sqcup(\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{2}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fun}}}{F}({X})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{(E_{1}\sqcup E_{2})}

\displaystyle(E_{1,1}~E_{1,2})\sqcup(E_{2,1}~E_{2,2})

\displaystyle\triangleq(E_{1,1}\sqcup E_{2,1})~(E_{1,2}\sqcup E_{2,2})

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{1}})\sqcup(\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{2}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\operatorname{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}tfun}}}{F}({\alpha})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{(E_{1}\sqcup E_{2})}

\displaystyle(E_{1}~t)\sqcup(E_{2}~t)

\displaystyle\triangleq(E_{1}\sqcup E_{2})~t

\displaystyle(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fold}}~{E_{1}})\sqcup(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fold}}~{E_{2}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fold}}~{(E_{1}\sqcup E_{2})}

\displaystyle(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}unfold}}~{E_{1}})\sqcup(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}unfold}}~{E_{2}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}unfold}}~{(E_{1}\sqcup E_{2})}

\displaystyle(E_{1,1},E_{1,2})\sqcup(E_{2,1},E_{2,2})

\displaystyle\triangleq((E_{1,1}\sqcup E_{2,1}),(E_{1,2}\sqcup E_{2,2}))

\displaystyle(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fst}}~{E_{1}})\sqcup(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fst}}~{E_{2}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fst}}~{(E_{1}\sqcup E_{2})}

\displaystyle(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}snd}}~{E_{1}})\sqcup(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}snd}}~{E_{2}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}snd}}~{(E_{1}\sqcup E_{2})}

\displaystyle(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{E_{1}})\sqcup(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{E_{2}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{(E_{1}\sqcup E_{2})}

\displaystyle(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{E_{1}})\sqcup(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{E_{2}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{(E_{1}\sqcup E_{2})}

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}case}}~{E_{1,1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}\\
\mid\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{X}\Rightarrow{E_{1,2}}\\
\mid\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{Y}\Rightarrow{E_{1,3}}\end{array}\right)\sqcup\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}case}}~{E_{2,1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}\\
\mid\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{X}\Rightarrow{E_{2,2}}\\
\mid\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{Y}\Rightarrow{E_{2,3}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}case}}~{(E_{1,1}\sqcup E_{2,1})}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}\\
\mid\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inl}}~{X}\Rightarrow{E_{1,2}\sqcup E_{2,2}}\\
\mid\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}inr}}~{Y}\Rightarrow{E_{1,3}\sqcup E_{2,3}}\end{array}

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}localCase}}~{E_{1,1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}\\
\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{E_{1,2}}\\
\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{E_{1,3}}\end{array}\right)\sqcup\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}localCase}}~{E_{2,1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}\\
\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{E_{2,2}}\\
\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{E_{2,3}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}localCase}}~{(E_{1,1}\sqcup E_{2,1})}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}of}}\\
\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inl}~{x}\Rightarrow{E_{1,2}\sqcup E_{2,2}}\\
\mid\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.4844,0.1289}inr}~{y}\Rightarrow{E_{1,3}\sqcup E_{2,3}}\end{array}

\displaystyle(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{1,1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{1,2}})\sqcup(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{2,1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2,2}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{x}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{(E_{1,1}\sqcup E_{2,1})}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{(E_{1,2}\sqcup E_{2,2})}

\displaystyle(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{1,1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{1,2}})\sqcup(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{E_{2,1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2,2}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{\alpha\mkern 2.0mu{::}\mkern 2.0mu\kappa}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}{(E_{1,1}\sqcup E_{2,1})}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{(E_{1,2}\sqcup E_{2,2})}

\displaystyle(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho})\sqcup(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{E_{2}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}send}}~{(E_{1}\sqcup E_{2})}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}to}}~{\rho}

\displaystyle(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\ell})\sqcup(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\ell})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}recv}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}from}}~{\ell}

\displaystyle(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choose}}~{d}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}for}}~{\ell}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}{E_{1}})\sqcup(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choose}}~{d}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}for}}~{\ell}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}{E_{2}})

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choose}}~{d}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}for}}~{\ell}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13};}}{(E_{1}\sqcup E_{2})}

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1,1}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{1,2}}\end{array}\right)\sqcup\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{2,1}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2,2}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}allow}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}choice}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}L}}\Rightarrow{E_{1,1}\sqcup E_{1,2}}\\
\mid\mathbf{{\color[rgb]{0.7344,0.4766,0}R}}\Rightarrow{E_{2,1}\sqcup E_{2,2}}\end{array}

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{r@{~}l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}~{\rho}&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1,1}}\\
&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{1,2}}\end{array}\right)\sqcup\left(\begin{array}[]{r@{~}l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}~{\rho}&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{2,1}}\\
&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2,2}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{array}[]{r@{~}l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}~{\rho}&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{(E_{1,1}\sqcup E_{2,1})}\\
&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{(E_{1,2}\sqcup E_{2,2})}\end{array}

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({E_{1,1}})\\
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{1,2}}\end{array}\right)\sqcup\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({E_{2,1}})\\
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{E_{2,2}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{array}[]{l}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}let}}~{(\alpha,x)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\coloneqq}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}fork}}({E_{1,1}\sqcup E_{2,1}})\\
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}in}}~{(E_{1,2}\sqcup E_{2,2})}\end{array}

\displaystyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}\sqcup\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}exit}}

D.2.
Endpoint Projection

Note that
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}~{\ell}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2}}
is shorthand for
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}AmI}}\mathord{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}\in}}~{\{\ell\}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}then}}~{E_{1}}~\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{1,0.23,0.13}else}}~{E_{2}}
.
